Scale brings its own limits. Medellín's Comuna 13 drew 1.98 million visitors in 2025, 52% of them
international, with about 300 professional guides. Mobility built for residents became the stage, but
carrying-capacity limits came late. On Vilakazi Street in Soweto, ten restaurants employ more than 800
people. Public space upgrades amplify existing assets, but benefits concentrate on street-front owners. 103

Neither capital nor donor money lasts without a seller that can stand alone. Costa Rica's community
network turned over more than USD 800,000 in 2013 and dissolved at the end of 2018; it did not survive
donor exit, though the record names no single cause. Kliptown in Soweto received a first allocation of
R436 million and was projected to draw almost 200,000 international visitors a year. By 2017 its museum
drew about 4,000 visitors a year, because capital built before an operator yields no operating income. 104

The cases converge on one order: "a seller with a stake, a safe and workable visit, product that keeps
spend in the community, then exposure". In the Western Cape's communities, the seller therefore comes
before the asset (section 2.4). In Doringbaai and Elands Bay, a local entity comes before more product,
with its own selling channel and a succession plan that does not rest on a donor. Assets follow proven
demand.105

The system matters more than any lever
No single lever explains the leaders' growth. Sustained tourism growth "is not created by fragmented
interventions, but by a coordinated destination system in which governance, demand generation and
access reinforce one another". Chile's coordination works best "when anchored around a single strategic
constraint". The Western Cape's constraint has the same shape as Chile's long-haul access: seats into
one gateway airport (section 2.3).106

Yet coordination is hard everywhere. Victoria reports tension between national and state bodies over roles
and branding. Valencia struggles to join its data across institutions. The Western Cape's three tiers of
government show the same strain (section 2.4). The province should anchor its plan on one constraint, as
Chile did: peak seats into Cape Town first, one system built around them and, in the communities, a seller
before any asset.107

What the comparison cannot show
The cases show what worked elsewhere; they cannot prove it will work here. Singapore's tourism board
answers to one ministry, in a system strongly directed by the state yet closely tied to the private sector.
The Western Cape works across three tiers, and air rights and visas sit with national government (section
2.4). New Zealand's governance differs too, and transfer means adapting governance, data and capability
to local realities.108

The evidence also has gaps. No score by criterion is printed, and among the six only New Zealand (3.67)
and Kenya (3.50) have a printed score. The margins behind the choice cannot be checked. No case of
failure was studied. The nearest local record is the City's own. Its Tourism Development Framework
reports uneven past progress and links it to restructuring, thin resources, little support from other
departments and weak monitoring. That is a plan, not a destination, so the gap remains. [Open item: add
one comparator that underperformed, as the study plan asked.] Several comparator charts are unlabelled,
so only printed figures are quoted here, and Valencia's figures name no primary source. The
recommended air access fund carries no amount. None of the cases holds Western Cape data, so the

2.2 Demand-side factors

Interest in the Western Cape is broad and growing. Yet demand is concentrated in five
markets and six months. The growth markets fly in through Gulf and African hubs and book
on platforms where Western Cape product is hard to find.

A 2025 values-based survey reached more than 25,000 travellers in 34 outbound markets, 17 of them
Wesgro priority markets, with 800 to 1,600 in each. More than 20 discussions with market-facing
stakeholders added judgement to desk review of Wesgro reports, Statistics South Africa and Google
search data.110

Five markets carry the province and pay among the most
The UK, Germany, the USA, the Netherlands and France supplied 63% of overseas arrivals in 2025, and
the first three alone 52%. The pattern is not new: the top five held 61% of Cape Town overseas air arrivals
109
    Inception Report, p31, p16; Benchmarking, p12, p24, p39, p46, p53, p28; the plan asked for four success cases and two that
underperformed; City of Cape Town, Tourism Development Framework 2019 to 2024, p3.
110
    Market Assessment, p40; Demand-Side Analysis, p7.
                                                                                                                   DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



from January to August 2024. Within the five, the USA is gaining. On the Provincial Treasury's count of all
international arrivals at Cape Town International, Africa included, its share rose 3.8 points on 2019, to
15.0% in 2024. The UK's and Germany's shares slipped by under a point and France's fell 1.6 points. That
count is wider than the overseas shares above.111

The same markets pay among the most. Average spend per visitor in 2024 ran from R32,900 for the USA
and R26,500 for the UK down to R8,600 for Namibia and R8,500 for Zimbabwe. Only Nigerians and
Ghanaians spend as much, from a far smaller base (below). High-spend visitors come from Europe and
North America, "tying future value growth to slower-growing source markets". The Provincial Treasury's
2024 regional averages are lower and rank the markets differently. Middle East visitors spend most, at
R23,900, then Europeans at R19,900, while North Americans spend R16,100 against R17,000 for all
international tourists. [Open item: SA Tourism to confirm the basis of the per-market spend figures, which
sit well above the Provincial Treasury's regional averages.] On both counts Europe spends more than
most, so the Western Cape must hold yield in its mature markets, Europe first, while volume growth
comes from elsewhere.112

Six months carry three fifths of demand
Demand peaks in the southern summer and thins in winter. December, January and November took
12.7%, 11.5% and 11.3% of overseas visitors in 2025, while June fell to 6.1%. October to March hold
about 62% of the year. The 2024 Wesgro Visitor Tracking Survey gives a similar split of 61% and 39%. 113

Growth therefore depends on "improving conversion in Apr-Sep rather than pushing incremental demand
into already busy peak months". Airlines cut seats in those same months, so filling the April to September
green season needs seats as well as conversion (section 2.3).114

The province converts a small share of a large market
Global demand is not the constraint. Over 2022 to 2024 the province received 3.6 million international
arrivals in total, about 1.2 million a year. That is about 0.1% of global tourist arrivals over the period and
0.05% of global tourist spend.115

Most Indian and Chinese visitors to South Africa never reach the province. About 27% of South Africa's
Indian arrivals and 29% of its Chinese arrivals came to the Western Cape over the three years, against
52% to 58% for the UK, Germany, France and the Netherlands. Those visitors are already in the country,
so they are a market to win alongside new routes. Among US travellers' destinations South Africa placed
second in Africa but 52nd to 57th worldwide over 2022 to 2024, so there it competes as an African choice
more than a global one.116

Removing barriers, not demand, sets the upside
The scenarios differ by intervention, not by demand: "the difference between scenarios is not demand
potential, but the extent to which structural barriers are removed". The growth model builds each market's
rate from four levers: outbound growth, the Western Cape's penetration, the airlift ceiling and structural
conditions such as currency and visas. It anchors on OAG aviation data for access and traveller data for
demand.117

From the 2025 base, the base case reaches 2,175,778 arrivals by 2035, implied growth of 3.8% a year.
The conservative case reaches 1,822,634, at 2.0%, and the accelerated case 2,516,282, at 5.3%. The
base case's 3.8% sits within the 3% to 5% norm, and only the accelerated case clears the 5% mark of
structural acceleration (section 2.1).118




Figure 2.2. Growth scenarios to 2035
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p19 (scenario end points) and p20 (base-case year points and the seat line as drawn). OAG
Aviation Analytics.

The step from 2,175,778 to 2,516,282 rests on confirmed direct routes to India and China, above-baseline
activation and more hub capacity into Cape Town. The dependency is hard: "without confirmed routes,
volume reverts to base". Those routes are not close. A direct Indian service is unlikely before 2030, and
China is a longer prize still. Until they come, the hub carriers carry the growth markets. Even the base
case passes the model's seat line at the 2030 year point (section 2.3).119

Hub markets add most arrivals, and India and China grow fastest
In the base case the hub markets add the most arrivals and the acceleration markets grow fastest. The
hub markets are the UAE, Ethiopia, Qatar and Turkey. The acceleration markets are the Gulf, China, India
and Brazil ([Open item: Skift to confirm which Gulf markets the acceleration group holds, since the UAE
and Qatar sit in the hub group.]). Between 2025 and 2035 hub markets add 206,584 arrivals, mature
markets 178,780, acceleration markets 175,818, regional markets 110,298 and Japan and South Korea
5,469.120

India more than doubles from 76,547 to 165,260 arrivals and China with Hong Kong from 43,412 to
92,598, both at 8% a year. Spend follows the mix. The model fixes average spend for each group, from
R24,500 a visitor in mature markets to R8,500 in regional ones. On that basis base-case foreign spend
rises from a modelled R27.12bn in 2024 to R40.59bn in 2035. Volume and value come from different
markets.121

The right segments can spread demand
Four values-based segments fit the Western Cape, together about 97 million addressable travellers. Each
has priority markets: Curious Explorers in the UK and the USA, and Creative Connectors in India.
Discerning Connoisseurs are paired with China and the UAE, and Reflective Adventurers with Germany. 122

Curious Explorers and Discerning Connoisseurs are "most aligned to the Western Cape's offering". The
segments also matter for spread, not only for volume. Curious Explorers offer the "strongest opportunity
for geographic dispersal beyond Cape Town"; Reflective Adventurers have "strong potential to support
seasonality mitigation" through year-round outdoor and wellness travel. These two are the segments to
target for the regions and the green season.123

India and China already arrive through hubs
The growth markets are already in the air, on other carriers. About 71,000 travellers from India and about
30,000 from China reach Cape Town each year through connecting hubs, together about 101,000
journeys. That count is of journeys by origin. SA Tourism counts about 28,000 Indian and Chinese tourist
arrivals a year in the province, so the two series measure different things (section 1.1). Addis Ababa and
Dubai carry 81% of the Indian travellers, and half of them start in Mumbai. Direct Indian service is "unlikely
before 2030 due to aircraft constraints at Air India and IndiGo". For China, a China Southern service from
Guangzhou is the likeliest first direct route.124

Australia (61,000 indirect travellers) and Saudi Arabia (137,000 passengers, with Riyadh Air's planned
hub) come next. The Western Cape should grow this hub traffic now. Singapore shows the method: its
tourism board, airport and airline work to one strategy (section 2.1). Here the partners are the hub carriers:
Emirates, Ethiopian, Qatar Airways and Turkish Airlines. Wesgro should build early relationships with the
Indian carriers and Indian government authorities. It should treat China as a long-term investment in
awareness and indirect volume before any non-stop service.125

Each market books on its own platforms
Conversion happens on platforms that differ by market. Chinese travellers discover on Douyin and
Xiaohongshu and book on Trip.com, Qunar and Fliggy. Indian travellers book through MakeMyTrip,
Booking.com and Expedia, and much Gulf travel still runs through private advisors and concierge
agencies.126

Each market also has its own gap. Cape Town has no direct flight to Mumbai or Delhi and thin Indian-
friendly adaptation, while Gulf travellers find no Arabic-language booking or local-currency pricing. Visas
add a further barrier, and they sit with national government (section 2.4).127

The province's own channels also lag. The Department of Economic Development rates destination
platforms behind international practice in online presence and AI adoption. The Overberg says its
proximity to Cape Town is not packaged before travellers book flights. The money should therefore follow
the platforms: activate inside them and shift investment "toward trade-led conversion rather than broad
awareness campaigns".128

Africa is a base and a growth market
Closer to home, Africa already supplies a large and steady base. The province recorded 323,971 tourist
arrivals from 16 reported African markets in 2024, 62% of them from Zimbabwe and Namibia. African
visitors spent R4.2bn in the province in 2025, and among them Nigerians and Ghanaians spent most per
visit, at R31,000 and R27,700. Namibia and Zimbabwe "add stability, repeat demand and counter-
seasonal volume".129

Nigeria, Kenya and Ghana are the priority growth markets, though the sources rank Ghana differently.
Nigeria's addressable market is modelled at 310,000 to 510,000 adult trips a year and Kenya's at 240,000
to 400,000. Africa is therefore both a regional foundation to keep and a source of high-spending visitors to
grow.130

Business events can fill the trough if bids aim at it
Business events run on a different calendar from leisure travel. International association meetings in Cape
Town rose from 33 in 2022 to 58 in 2024. Of the 136 held over the three years, 109 fell between May and
November, but 74 fell in September to November alone. Most meetings therefore land at the edge of the
trough, not in it. Cape Town ranked 35th in the 2024 ICCA ranking and hosted 37 of South Africa's 64
UIA-recorded meetings in 2025. Hotel occupancy in Cape Town was 53% in June 2023, the gap such
events could fill.131

Yet MICE is not one business. Meetings, incentives, conventions and exhibitions have different buyers
and lead times. Only conferences are measured well, and meetings are "absent from consolidated
provincial statistics". Stellenbosch and George are credible secondary hubs that can take delegates' extra
nights while major events stay in Cape Town. The lever needs one events calendar, as Melbourne has
(section 2.1), bids aimed at May to August and measures for each of its four businesses. 132

Domestic travel has not recovered
Domestic demand gives the province no cushion. The province recorded 3.458 million domestic trips in
2024, 6.1% fewer than at the start of 2020. Provinces such as the Eastern Cape drew more of the budget-
conscious recovery. The mandate here is international, but growth that lifts peak prices could crowd out
the domestic visitors on whom the green season and the regions lean. The action plan should therefore
track domestic trips as context.133

Pillar 1 must fill the green season, win growth markets on the routes and platforms they already use, hold
yield in mature markets and build the African base. Seats, regional product and the enabling environment
(sections 2.3 and 2.4) decide whether that demand lands.

      FA06, Build a regional foundation (Africa). A large regional base and two high-yield growth
      markets.




2.3 Supply-side factors

Supply sets the ceiling on growth. The gateway's busiest direct routes run full, the new
runway is years away and airlines are expected to raise fares before they add seats. Beyond
the Metro the product exists but is hard to reach, book or stay in.

Stakeholders rate supply constraints the most severe: 44 of the 68 insights on supply are rated High,
against 23 of 64 on demand. Two bodies of evidence test that view. Scheduled seats and estimated
bookings cover every Cape Town route, with bookings from the airlines' global distribution systems to
November 2025. On the ground, 50,332 tourism assets are mapped across six regions in three layers:
attractions, services and enabling infrastructure. The map draws on Wesgro's supply database, SANParks
and CapeNature data and open geographic sources. Its premise is that "destination performance is
constrained by the weakest layer". [Dataset limits, including the absence of geography below district level,
to be confirmed against the supply dataset notes.] Each region is scored from 1 to 5 on attractiveness
(40%), readiness and capacity (30%) and accessibility (30%).134

The model hits its seat line by 2030
On the growth model, seats run out before demand does. The model draws current air access as "a hard
ceiling at 1.8 million inbound seats". The base case passes that line by 2030, at 1,802,929 arrivals, up
from 1,673,855 in 2028. The line is the model's assumption, not a measured cap; what binds in practice is
the runway at peak hours (section 1.3). On the model's own judgement, even 2.1 million arrivals by 2035,
below the base case (section 2.2), needs deliberate intervention to add capacity. Growth to 2.5 million is
therefore a supply programme before it is a marketing one.135

Infrastructure Report, p2, p4, p9, p10, p17, p18; the scores "are evidence informed, not purely formula derived" and missing data is not
scored as poor performance.

Cape Town's routes already run full
Cape Town has won more routes than its peers (section 2.1), and they now run full. Twenty-nine
international routes serve the airport in 2026, and Cape Town's share of South Africa's international
capacity rose from 10% in 2010 to 25%. In the year to November 2025, 11 of the 27 routes with load data
ran above 80%. Newark led at 88.6%, ahead of Atlanta at 84.3% and Frankfurt and London Heathrow at
83.0%. Sixteen ran at 75% or more.136




Figure 2.4. Route load factors to November 2025
Source: MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment, Mar 2026, "Seasonality" (international route load-factor table: estimated bookings divided by
capacity, bookings for the twelve months to November 2025). Routes above 80% highlighted; the eleven routes below 75% are not shown.

Across all its routes, domestic included, Cape Town's 79% load factor is the joint highest of South Africa's
five largest airports, with Lanseria. South Africa averages about 70% and Africa 75%, and Cape Town's
international routes alone average 76%. Regional routes also run full, and all five markets are named for
more capacity. Luanda ran at 86.2% and 83.4% on its two airports, Maputo 83.3%, Gaborone 82.3%,
Mauritius 81.8% and Harare 81.3%. Domestically, Cape Town to Durban runs at 90.0% and warrants
more seats, while other domestic routes sit between 70% and 80%.137

Cape Town has reached "a 'what next' moment". Near-term seats must come from more frequencies and
larger aircraft on routes already full. Beyond that, the province needs a route strategy that "looks for long-
term gains rather than short-term wins".138

The network runs through hubs and skips Asia
Cape Town's seven largest international markets are all hub airports, with 2.7 million seats in 2026 against
2.0 million in 2019. Western Europe holds 38% of international capacity, the Middle East 23%, Southern
Africa 17%, Eastern Africa 14% and North America 6%. Most hub passengers connect: Emirates'
connecting share is "above 80% annually" and Qatar Airways' is nine in ten.139

New carriers lean towards Africa and the hubs. Seven of the twelve that launched Cape Town services
since 2014 fly to African points. By 2026 seats the largest are Airlink to regional points (441,602) and
Ethiopian to Addis Ababa (437,430). Turkish to Istanbul (240,200), United to Newark and Washington
(172,190) and Kenya Airways to Nairobi (127,138) follow.140

Yet no seat flies direct to Asia: the network shows "the absence of any direct capacity to any of the Asian
markets". Thirteen of the 27 routes in the load table serve African or Indian Ocean points, and none
serves Asia. A direct Asian route is therefore a long-term prize, and the near-term seats sit with the hub
carriers. The hub routes are not full. Dubai ran at 72.3%, Addis Ababa at 71.9% and Doha at 65.2% in the
year to November 2025. On those routes marketing can still fill seats, which it cannot do on the eleven full
ones.141

Airlines cut winter seats and may raise fares
On full routes, airlines are expected to raise fares before they add seats. The forecast is that "carriers will
seek to increase revenues via higher selling fares rather than add new services", which "will frustrate that
desire for increased visitors". No fare data backs the forecast; it rests on judgement of airline behaviour. In
winter carriers withdraw seats. June carried 6.4% of Cape Town's 2025 international seats and December
10.8%, with May, July, August and September at 6.9% each.142




Figure 2.5. International seats by month, 2025
Source: MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment, Mar 2026, "Seasonality" (Capetown international capacity by month, 2025, printed data labels).

Connecting traffic is "especially critical in the low season", which ties the winter schedule to the hub
carriers' own priorities. New seats must therefore be won route by route and season by season, as New
Zealand did with route-tied marketing (section 2.1).143

Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035                                              DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



One main runway caps the gateway
The airport, not the airlines, sets the outer limit. Cape Town International's one main runway has no
parallel taxiway and caps movements at about 30 an hour. Two of the airport's seven parts are already
full. The R6.39bn runway relocation lifts the cap to 45 movements an hour, a 50% rise. On the planning
timeline it completes around 2033, with terminal relief from about 2032. ACSA's latest schedule is earlier,
with the runway finished by December 2029, subject to contract award. This report assumes about 2033
and treats the earlier date as upside.144

The alternatives arrive sooner but carry less. George handled 911,000 passengers in 2025, above its
design limit. A R310m terminal expansion doubles its capacity to about 2 million passengers by 2028 or
2029. Cape Winelands Airport targets first passengers in 2028 on an R8bn first phase but has no firm
airline. Capacity and certainty pull apart: "the certain fix is the latest, and the earliest big-capacity option is
the least certain".145

Yet no stakeholder named Cape Town International's capacity as a constraint, and it appears only as a
transfer-time reference. No airline or airport sat among the 16 organisations, so the runway's absence
from their concerns is a gap in the record, not a finding. The outer limit is a national lever, held by ACSA
(section 2.4).146

Cruise needs product, not berths
Cape Town's cruise berth has room, and port spending is not going to cruise. In 2024/25 the berth
handled 83 calls against a season capacity of 150 or more, with about 127,000 passengers. Its limits are a
300m ship length, the South-Easter wind and no shore power. Wesgro's plan targets 250 calls over FY26
to FY30, about 50 a year.147

Cruise value comes from the mix of passengers, not their number. International passengers rose from
55,495 in 2022/23 to 100,255 in 2024/25, about 81% more. In the last season they grew 39%, against
16% for all passengers. Section 1.2 sets out what the season added to provincial GDP-R and jobs. The
base is volatile: reroutes made 20 of the 67 calls in 2023/24.148

Mossel Bay handled 21 calls and about 13,000 passengers and opened a R22m passenger facility in
November 2025. Ships anchor and tender because it has no cruise quay, and only small and mid-sized
ships call. Saldanha Bay has had 3 trial calls and shares the ore terminal, with no cruise berth or
passenger facility. Its R4bn port upgrade serves iron ore. Mossel Bay's own count differs: calls rose from
four before COVID to 27 a season. StratEcon's study for Wesgro counts 21 passenger-day visits there in
2024/25, with 31,146 passengers and R46.3m of spending. [Open item: reconcile the three Mossel Bay
counts against each source's definitions.]149

Mossel Bay is ready for day stops, so the stance is to protect Cape Town, back Mossel Bay now and
watch Saldanha Bay. The province should grow cruise "through product and marketing, not capital
works".150

The flagships are full in season
The province's best-known sites run at capacity in summer. At the Table Mountain Aerial Cableway, peak
queues of up to three hours up and two down detract from the visitor experience. Chapman's Peak suffers
disruptions, and the peak over December and January "overwhelms transport and accommodation
capacity". Boulders is "already near carrying capacity", and summit expansion on Table Mountain is
severely limited. Yet the township attractions are underused, and the overflow reserves are under-
marketed. CapeNature's reserves are already drawing more visitors. Over the festive season, Robberg's
day visitors rose 50% and Stony Point's visitors 28%. Stony Point, the Overberg's penguin colony, is this
report's alternative to Boulders. On the peninsula the Southern Line through Muizenberg, Kalk Bay and
Simon's Town is "a ready-made, low-cost scenic corridor". Eleven of the 16 organisations see the offer as
concentrated around Cape Town and a narrow set of icons. The peak must therefore spread to product
with room, under the capacity discipline New Zealand applies (section 2.1). Beyond a few reserves, the
regions cannot yet take the overflow.151

Cape Point is a flagship in its own right. Counted as the Cape of Good Hope, it drew 940,399 visitors in
2023, up 49.0%. More than half of international tourists, 55.4%, go there. No source yet measures the
carrying capacity of any flagship.152

Readiness and access fall away from the Metro
Overall readiness halves between the Metro and the periphery. The Cape Metro rates 4.5 out of 5, the
Winelands and the Garden Route 3.4, the Overberg 2.7, the West Coast 2.5 and the Central Karoo 1.9.
Accessibility falls fastest. On attractions it scores 4.7 in the Metro, 3.3 in the Winelands and 3.0 on the
Garden Route. It drops to 2.0 in the Overberg, 1.7 on the West Coast and 1.0 in the Central Karoo, while
the Metro scores 5.0 on transport.153

Cape Town is both the gateway and the dispersal point. The Metro holds 20,281 of the 50,332 assets
(40.3%) and five of the province's six highest-volume attractions. It is the only region with an international
airport and rapid transit. It also generates 72.5% of provincial output, measured as GDPR. Visitors are
more concentrated than assets. Nine of the ten places international tourists visit most are in the Metro, led
by the V&A Waterfront, which 78.3% of them visit. In 2023 the Metro's attractions also outgrew the
province's, while Garden Route visitor numbers fell 20%.154

Beyond the Metro the visitor drives. Even the Garden Route, with its airport, has no shuttle between
towns. Public transport is "effectively absent" on the West Coast and in the Overberg, and the Winelands
have no transit between estates or valleys. Every accessibility measure in the Central Karoo "sits at the
floor", and even Metro rail is "functionally unusable for tourism". Stakeholders call weak public transport,
congestion and unreliable last-mile access "structural barriers to visitor movement within and beyond
Cape Town". Dispersal therefore "needs deliberate corridor investment, not organic spillover from Cape
Town".155

Strong regional assets lack beds and booking
The assets that could draw visitors outward sit where few can stay or book. The West Coast has the
province's highest natural-asset attractiveness and the Central Karoo its most unique niche assets, yet
both score lowest on readiness. Inland areas north of the N2 on the Garden Route are tourism-inactive.
Industrial belts in the Metro show "zero tourism conversion" despite nearby attractions. 156

Beds are one gap. In the Winelands mid-market accommodation "is effectively absent outside
Stellenbosch", and a "luxury-skewed product mix excludes mid-market and family segments".
Accommodation is thin beyond Langebaan and Paternoster on the West Coast and thin at international
grade along Route 62. On the West Coast, where beds are thin, overnight stays remain below the
provincial average. No bed count yet sizes the gap.157

Booking is the second gap, and it is widest in the regions. Six touring routes were rated Ready,
Developing or Gap on access, accommodation, safety and bookability (Emerging is the overall rating as
printed for the West Coast route). Route 62 has the lowest online bookability of the six, and the West
Coast route rates Gap on digital distribution and payments. CapeNature takes about 70% of its
reservations through its call centre rather than its online platform. Even in the Metro, about 40% or more of
smaller operators lack online booking.158

No committed money closes the regional gaps
Committed capital closes the air gaps, not the regional ones. The Metro's pipeline exceeds R30bn, led by
ACSA's R11.3bn airport overhaul, the R20bn V&A Granger Bay expansion and the R3.3bn MyCiTi Phase
2. Outside the Metro the money goes to roads, ports, airports and a few precincts: Cape Winelands
Airport, the George terminal, the R693m Malmesbury bypass, the R62 reconstruction and the R81.7m
Agulhas lighthouse precinct.159

None of it buys transit between towns, mid-market beds or a booking route. Section 2.4 shows that the
bodies that manage and sell the regions are short of money themselves.

Pillar 2 must therefore win seats into the gateway first, then build the corridors, beds and booking that
decide where growth lands.

      What this means for the focus areas
      FA07, Win direct routes and add seats on full routes. Eleven routes run above 80% and fares are
      likely to rise before seats. The runway is the binding constraint until about 2033, and the base case
      passes the model's seat line by 2030. Read as a rise in yearly arrivals by 2030, Wesgro's FY26 to
      FY30 arrivals target is about 2.6 to 3.3 times the base case's gain (section 1.1).
      FA08, Grow cruise calls and ports. Cruise needs product, not berths. Value grows with the
      international mix rather than the number of calls.
      FA07 and FA15, Crowd in capital with targeted investment strategies. Airport capital is
      committed, but regional gaps have no committed capital.
      FA09, Accelerate tourism-enabling internal mobility. Accessibility falls from 4.7 to 1.0, and public
      transport is absent beyond the Metro. Stakeholders name last-mile access as a barrier, which
      supports it. The Department of Mobility's draft Tourism Transport Strategy already plans the airport
      link and tourist route signage (section 2.4).
      FA10, Support diversified route and product development, and FA11, Align visitor experience
      in-destination. Strong assets sit in areas with little tourism use, and mid-market beds are missing.
      Stakeholders' account of a narrow product set supports FA10, and nine of the ten places international
      tourists visit most are in the Metro.
      FA12, Balance impact on nature-based attractions. Flagships are full at peak, as stakeholders
      also report. Table Mountain National Park alone drew 2.8 million visitors in 2023. The underused
      alternatives are the township attractions, the receiving reserves such as Stony Point and the Southern
      Line towns.
      FA03, Make the whole province findable and bookable. Bookability is weakest in the regions, and
      stakeholders report product that cannot be booked online.
      FA02, FA04, FA05 and FA06, from the air side. Winter seats fall to 6.4% of the year in June. No
      seat flies direct to Asia today, and the hub routes still have seats, so conversion runs through the
      hubs. The mature long-haul routes are the fullest, and routes to five regional markets run above 80%
      and are named for more capacity.




2.4 The enabling environment

The enabling system is thinnest where the action plan needs it most. The decisive levers sit
nationally, local tourism bodies' funding has been disrupted and the province has no
approved way to count its tourism jobs. In the communities, organisation rather than
distance keeps visitor spend out.
159
   Infrastructure Report, p32, p43, p54, p65, p76, p87; the pipeline also holds a wider Winelands airport precinct of R16bn to R20bn, a R9.4m
landing-system upgrade at George, the R17m Malgas road, the Saldanha port upgrade and rural broadband to 2026.


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                       58
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035                                            DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



The binding levers sit with national government
The levers that most limit growth are held nationally. In an insight rated High, the Department of Economic
Development reports that "restrictive bilateral air agreements are limiting airlift growth", leaving the
province less competitive than peers. Chinese routes are "frequently an extension of government-to-
government relations". The runway that caps Cape Town International sits with ACSA, a national body
(sections 1.2 and 2.3).160

Visas and national marketing follow the same pattern. Chinese and Gulf travellers face slow, document-
heavy visa processes. Four organisations raised visas, among them the Cableway for India and China
and the CTICC for African events. The Department of Economic Development also finds that SA
Tourism's institutional weakness constrains international marketing. The Western Cape holds none of
these levers (section 1.2), so it must move them by escalation backed with evidence. Chile's case points
the same way: lobby for air service liberalisation and simpler visas (section 2.1).161

The province holds instruments for that escalation. Its own visa study, by BDO for the Department of
Economic Development, ranks the reforms to press for: published processing times first, then visa
exemption for China, India and Nigeria. Wesgro's Advocacy and Advisory unit exists to press such
evidence on the levers the province does not control (section 1.2). The Tourism Blueprint 2030 names the
same barriers: poor continental air access from restricted markets, a costly and inconvenient visa regime
and persistent safety concerns.162

Funding has thinned and mandates blur
The bodies that manage and sell the destination are weaker than a decade ago. The Department of
Economic Development rates Western Cape and Cape Town tourism funding "materially below peer
destinations" for destination management and marketing. It finds that tourism institutional capacity "has
eroded over the past decade at both provincial and municipal level", and both findings are rated High. 163

National Treasury's MFMA Circular No. 131 of 31 March 2025 has since disrupted established municipal
funding for local tourism organisations (LTOs), a finding also rated High. Most of them "are not
operationally sustainable without municipal funding support". Mandates also blur at district level. The West
Coast District Municipality reports "a mandate disconnect" between the district, local municipalities and
LTOs that leaves tourism development without a clear owner.164

Fiscal pressure is turning attention to a tourism levy and blended finance. The Department of Mobility's
draft transport framework plans its own business case for a tourist levy, as alternative funding for tourism
transport. The committed capital in section 2.3 builds airports, ports, roads, transit and precincts, but none
of it funds the bodies that manage and sell the destination. The plan cannot rest on LTOs until their
funding and mandates are settled.165

Climate losses already fall on the flagships
Climate risk is now an operating cost at Robben Island and in CapeNature's reserves. Robben Island
Museum loses about R16 million a year to weather-cancelled trips, a High-rated insight. CapeNature
reports climate-related flooding, fire and heat closing sites. Sustainable destination management is
therefore risk management for the province's flagship product today, and should be funded as such, not as
a later add-on.166

Collaboration platforms exist; data does not flow
Collaboration and data sharing are the barriers stakeholders raise most often. Fifteen of the 16
organisations raised industry and government collaboration, and fifteen raised policy and data sharing.167




Figure 2.7. Focus areas stakeholders raised
Source: Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026, p20 to p46: number of the 16 organisations with at least one insight in each category the
appendix tables code, shown against the focus area it bears on (computed). Routes and cruise are coded as one category, and none covers
skills. The report prints no tally.

Data sharing also draws 30 insights and 16 High ratings, the most of any category. Stakeholders describe
the data architecture as "too fragmented, lagged and proxy-based to provide a reliable view of visitor
volumes, spend, spread or economic impact at provincial level".168

Coordination is hard everywhere, as the comparators show (section 2.1). Yet the platforms for
collaboration already exist here. FEDHASA points to JAMS, through which tourism associations
coordinate with Wesgro, the Western Cape Government and the City of Cape Town. Cape Town Air
Access joins them, with ACSA and industry, around routes (section 1.2). What is missing is an agreed way
to share data across those bodies, not another forum.169

Transport and safety plans exist
Two plans reach into the visitor's journey. The Department of Mobility's draft Provincial Land Transport
Framework includes a Tourism Transport Strategy, which plans to revive the MyCiTi airport service with
ACSA and to sign and maintain tourist routes. The City's framework for 2019 to 2024 proposed a Tourist
Safety Programme. It sets out secure zones in higher-risk tourism areas, a Tourist Police unit, victim
support and safety forums. The action plan should build on both plans rather than start new ones. [Open
item: the City of Cape Town and the Department of Mobility to confirm which parts of these plans are
adopted, funded or running.]170

Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035                                        DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



The province has no approved jobs count
The province cannot yet measure tourism's jobs or its contribution to GDP. The national Department of
Tourism reports that "there is no approved official methodology for provincial tourism GDP and jobs
estimates", rated High. Stats SA cites 749 vacancies behind delays to the tourism satellite account and
infers provincial flows partly from ports-of-entry data.171

The communities show the same gap at small scale. No community profile counts tourism employment
inside its boundary. The figures are node-side instead, such as about 4,700 formal Town Centre jobs for
82,393 employed Mitchells Plain residents. Need rests on fifteen-year-old Census data. CapeNature, the
West Coast District and the Overberg raise skills gaps, but no labour analysis sits behind them. 172

For the Western Cape, a jobs baseline must come before a jobs target. The ambition of 300,000 tourism-
linked jobs (section 1.1) has no baseline yet. The yield-based regional metrics that New Zealand
recommends (section 2.1) also need a reliable view of spend and spread, which today's data cannot give.

Communities hold people, not spend
Foreign visitors spent R25.9bn in the province in FY2025, but little of it reaches its townships and rural
settlements. The 20 shortlisted communities come from a long-list of every Census sub-place cluster of
1,500 people or more with official unemployment of 25% or more. Named mission, fishing and farm
settlements are added. That gives 107 communities holding 2.81 million people in 2011, 48% of the
province. Five weighted criteria (need, tourism potential, proximity, readiness and access) produce the
shortlist of 20, five in the Cape Metro and three in each district.173

The 20 hold 18.4% of the province's residents and 15.4% of employed residents (Census 2011), yet
receive 0.02% to 0.04% of foreign direct spend. This comes to about R4m to R10m a year on the
documented floor, against R259m a year if they captured 1% of FY2025 foreign direct spend. 174

Inclusion here means activity inside the community. A tour, a meal, a bed or a market counts; a resident
commuting to a hotel job in town does not. Inclusion therefore turns on where visitor spend lands, not on
how many visitors come.175

Organisation, not distance, keeps spend out
Distance is not the barrier. Of the 107 long-listed communities, 93 lie within 45 minutes of one of 35
tourism nodes or three corridors. Langa is 12 minutes from Cape Town International and Zwelihle seven
from Hermanus. Distance is the binding constraint in none of the 20. Yet proximity and access carried
35% of the shortlist score, so the result is partly built into the selection (Appendix A5, "Community shortlist
criteria").176

Organisation is the binding constraint in 11 of the 20. Ten have no identified active tourism body, and nine
show no benefit visible to residents.177

Figure 2.8. Binding constraints in 20 communities
Source: SME and Community Inclusion Plan, Sep 2026, p30 (constraint tally across the 20 shortlisted communities).

Product often exists without a seller. Langa's walk sells on global platforms at ratings above 4.8, yet city
firms sell most of the 23 Langa walks on Viator. Zwelihle's four resident guides sell by phone with no
shared booking channel. The Mitchells Plain festival drew 27,800 visitors in November 2025 with no local
operator or LTO behind it. The communities cannot yet be found or booked on the channels visitors use.
What they lack is a local seller, not a shorter journey.178

Safety is the second gate
After a seller, safety is the next gate a visit must pass. Nine of the 20 communities record a safety-
perception gap and five record extortion. Seven stakeholder organisations raised safety, a barrier that
runs across transport, township tourism and international selling. Tourism cannot fix this alone: "tourism
activity alone cannot resolve crime or extortion". Safety is therefore a launch requirement in its own right,
not an outcome of the pilot. No community pilot should open until it is met.179

Each community type needs a different first move
The largest group of long-listed communities sits near a node and has something worth visiting, and each
type needs its own first move. Near a node with assets underused, 49 long-listed and 11 shortlisted
communities need sellers organised and a slot in the node's itineraries, procurement and events. Near a
node with nothing inside, 43 long-listed and 6 shortlisted communities need a seller first, then one anchor
asset under a covenant, then integration.180

Remote communities with underused assets, 7 long-listed and 2 shortlisted, need access opened and the
asset placed on a route product the LTO already sells (Atlantis is classed remote by Wesgro's naming,
though it lies 26 minutes from Darling). Remote communities with nothing inside, 8 long-listed and 1
shortlisted, need procurement and skills first and an asset only once a route product exists. The first move
differs by type, but in each the seller or the route comes before the asset.181

A seller with a stake comes first
The evidence points to a model built around a seller. It joins a functioning selling capability to demand the
node already has, behind launch gates for safety, access and a viable operating model. A pilot in two or
three of the six communities profiled first would test it. The six are Langa, Kayamandi, Zwelihle, De Rust,
Doringbaai and Laingsburg, one in each region. It would scale only once bookings, local spend, delivery
and safety meet thresholds agreed before launch.182

The first output is deliberately small: "one live booking route, published operating dates, a named
customer contact and a simple record of bookings and local revenue". Most first moves carry no dedicated
budget line. Chapter 4 sets out roles and funding. The order the cases support (section 2.1) therefore
becomes the order of delivery: a seller first, then a safe visit, then product and exposure. 183

Every gap in this part is a question of who benefits, who pays, who decides or who measures. Pillar 3
must settle those four questions, because Pillars 1 and 2 cannot deliver without the answers.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035                DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




  What this means for the focus areas
  FA13, Embed local destination bodies for community inclusion. The binding constraint is a seller
  with a stake, then a safe way in, then demand pulled from the adjacent node.
  FA14, Manage climate risk and sustainable tourism, and FA15, Crowd in capital with targeted
  investment strategies. Unfunded local tourism bodies and climate losses at flagship sites. The
  Department of Mobility proposes its own tourist levy case.
  FA10, Support diversified route and product development, and FA15. Assets follow sellers and
  route products, not the reverse.
  FA16, Align institutional stakeholders across all three tiers. Air rights, visas and SA Tourism sit
  nationally, Chinese routes follow government-to-government agreements and airport capacity sits
  with ACSA. Cape Town Air Access joins the three tiers, ACSA and industry around routes.
  FA19, Lead removal of infrastructure, visa and safety deficits, a governance task because
  visas and air rights sit nationally. Visas slow Chinese and Gulf travellers, and safety and extortion
  gate activation in up to nine communities. The province's visa study ranks the reforms to press for,
  and the City's proposed Tourist Safety Programme is a base to build on where it runs.
  FA17, Strengthen industry and government collaboration, and FA18, Expedite tourism-
  enabling policy and data sharing. Each raised by 15 of 16 organisations, with existing platforms
  such as JAMS to build on.
  FA20, Develop workforce and service skills, waits on analysis still to be commissioned. Skills
  gaps raised by CapeNature, the West Coast District and the Overberg District have no labour
  analysis behind them. No count of tourism jobs exists, which also supports FA18.
  FA03, Make the whole province findable and bookable. Community product sells by phone or
  through city operators.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035                                   DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




3.1 Pillar 1: Reshape demand
Latent interest is not what holds Pillar 1 back; turning it into bookings is. The province is
known in its mature markets, under-sold in its growth markets and hard to book outside its
core.

Pillar 1 sets out to break through three ceilings on demand, in markets, seasons and spread, by reshaping
the brand toward new traveller segments. Five markets supplied 63% of overseas arrivals in 2025, and the
UK, Germany and the USA supplied 52% between them. June held 6.4% of the year's international seats
at Cape Town International in 2025, against 10.8% in December. Beyond the core routes, most rural
properties have no OTA presence and are invisible to the market.184

The gap between interest and arrival is wide. About 97 million high-value travellers in 34 markets have an
interest in South Africa, and about 1.4 million convert into arrivals. In the growth markets, "the constraint is
conversion and access, not only awareness". Each ceiling therefore needs a path from interest to booking:
green-season seats and events, bookable product and hub traffic that stops in Cape Town.185

Two themes carry the pillar. The first, traveller, brand and proposition, decides whom the province sells to,
when and how. Its focus areas target four segments in their home markets, build green-season demand
from business events and make the whole province bookable (focus areas 1 to 3). The second theme,
source market growth and diversification, decides which markets grow the base. It converts Indian and
Chinese travellers already flying in through hubs, raises yield in the mature markets and builds the
regional African base (focus areas 4 to 6).

The two themes work as one. The segments set the target inside each market, and the market groups set
where volume and value come from. The green season and bookability decide whether the demand they
win lands in the months and places with room to take it. Initiatives I1 to I5 carry the six focus areas, and
section 4.3 sets out each one.

Pillar 1 carries most of the base case. Between 2025 and 2035 the base case adds 676,949 arrivals. The
four hub markets, the UAE, Qatar, Ethiopia and Turkey, supply 206,584 of them and the mature markets
178,780. The acceleration markets, led by India and China, supply 175,818, regional Africa 110,298 and
the strategic group 5,469. None of it lands without seats, so the air access focus area in Pillar 2 runs
beside this pillar from the first phase (focus area 7). The Board will judge the pillar on arrivals by market
group, direct foreign spend and the green-season share, not on arrivals alone (section 5.2).186

3.1.1 Traveller, brand and proposition
The first theme is about whom the Western Cape sells to, when and how. It holds three focus areas: the
segments, the green season and the bookability of the province. Together they decide whether interest
becomes a trip: the right travellers, in months with room and on product they can book.

THEME 1.1 · TRAVELLER, BRAND AND PROPOSITION · FOCUS AREA 1


Respond to growth segment traveller preferences
Four traveller segments, each concentrated in named home markets, give Wesgro a sharper
target than country alone, and the province scores 3.7 to 4.6 out of 5 on its ability to win
them.

The opportunity
Country is too blunt a target for a long-haul destination. Within one market, travellers differ in what they
want from a trip, what they spend and where they book. Four values-based segments fit the Western
Cape. Together they hold about 97 million addressable travellers, and each is concentrated in named
home markets. Marketing to a segment inside a market puts each rand where the province is most likely
to win.187

The prize to 2035 is value as much as volume. Curious Explorers and Creative Connectors together hold
over 40% of global outbound leisure demand, while Discerning Connoisseurs deliver the highest economic
impact per arrival. The segments also help with season and spread. Curious Explorers offer the strongest
opportunity for dispersal beyond Cape Town, and Reflective Adventurers support seasonality mitigation
through year-round outdoor and wellness travel.188

The measure holds the province back as much as the message. Performance is judged on arrivals. The
Department of Economic Development says current growth framing "overemphasises visitor volumes" and
that a value and spend lens may suit better.189

in the currency printed, which the documents do not name. The 1.4 million carries no year or source line.

The four segments rest on a May 2025 survey of more than 25,000 outbound travellers in 34 markets,
applied to 17 priority markets. Each segment has its own home markets, spend and booking channels, so
one campaign cannot reach all four. China holds the largest pool, about 21.49 million, but air access and
visas constrain its conversion.190

Yet two limits apply. The survey leaves out the domestic market and seven African markets, among them
Nigeria, Kenya and Ghana. Its findings are directional rather than definitive, and no Western Cape
validation exists yet. Stakeholders also name interests outside the four: medical tourism, township culture,
eco-adventure and international MICE. These are product interests that cut across the segments, not a
fifth segment.191

The course of action
Wesgro should market by segment within market and judge results by value as well as volume. The UK
and Germany anchor the near term with a combined pool of about 12.5 million, and the USA is the largest
single pool outside China. The same research that tests the segments here should extend them to the
domestic and African markets, which the survey leaves out. The City's framework for 2019 to 2024 set out
four segments of its own: Indulgers, Explorers, Specialists and business-events delegates.192

The order matters. Segment targets and validation come first, in 2027. The findings are directional, and
the budget should not move on them at scale until they hold for the Western Cape (section 4.3). Spend
moves to each segment's own channels from 2028. Spend per visitor joins Wesgro's targets from 2029,
once the data exists to track it.

Why it can work
Segment targeting works because it moves money to where incidence, fit and channel meet. Curious
Explorers are 30% of UK and US travellers, and the province scores 4.6 out of 5 on its ability to win them.
Discerning Connoisseurs book 42% of trips through luxury travel advisors and 6% through OTAs. Broad
awareness misses them, while advisor partnerships reach them at the point of sale.193

New Zealand gives each priority market a role, with the USA as its high-yield growth engine. It judges
performance on revenue per visitor and length of stay over arrivals. Valencia matches product to purpose

THEME 1.1 · TRAVELLER, BRAND AND PROPOSITION · FOCUS AREA 2


Create green-season demand with MICE and events
The green season is a seat problem and an events problem at once. June holds 6.4% of the
year's international seats, and business events are the lever best placed to fill the trough,
though international meetings now peak from September to November.

The challenge
The green season is where the province has room to grow. Capacity sits underused in the green season
and across the regions. Cape Town hotel occupancy was 53% in June 2023. Growth to 2035 depends on
"improving conversion in Apr-Sep rather than pushing incremental demand into already busy peak
months".195

Yet the trough is a seat problem as well as a demand problem. Airlines cut seats in the same months that
visitors fall away, and connecting traffic is "especially critical in the low season". Leisure marketing alone
cannot fill a month that carriers have already thinned (section 2.3). The province needs reasons to travel
that are fixed in the calendar, booked months ahead and sold with the seats.196

Business events are the best of those reasons. They can be scheduled more flexibly than leisure trips, so
they can strengthen demand in shoulder and green-season periods. Two gaps remain: aim and
measurement. International meetings cluster just after the trough, and only conferences are counted well
(section 2.2).197

Figure 3.1. Meetings, seats and visitors by month

Seats and visitors both fall away from April to September. International meetings run thin from June to
August and peak from September to November, at the edge of the trough rather than in it. Events
therefore have one job: win new dates in May to September, where seats and visitors are thinnest.

Two limits qualify the numbers. The October to March share is about 62% for 2025, computed from a
month-of-travel table whose shares sum to 104.9% and normalised. The only printed split is 61% to 39%
for 2024, and this report carries both. And only conferences are measured: meetings are absent from
provincial statistics, incentive volumes are unverified and exhibitions are unquantified.198

The course of action
Wesgro should build an April to September events spine and sell it with the seats. Its plan sets 161
business event bids over FY26 to FY30, and the spine decides when they land. The spine rests on a small
set of flagship weeks, with green-season flight, stay and experience packages sold through airline and
trade partners. Bids follow the buyer. Conference bids aim at off-peak dates in sectors where the province
is strong. Incentive packages test the cooler season with Indian and Gulf planners, and winter midweek
packages target corporate meetings.199

The shared strategy and the count come first, in 2027. Wesgro agrees one event acquisition and calendar
strategy with the Convention Bureau, the City and the Province. The calendar strategy builds on the year-
round promotion and MICE marketing in the City's framework for 2019 to 2024. Wesgro also sets up a
way to measure meetings, incentives and exhibitions. Without that count, no one can tell whether the
calendar fills the trough. It then publishes the calendar and aims bids at May to September (section 4.3). 200

Why it can work
Association conferences are won with long lead times, and they bring visitors who might not otherwise
consider the province. A congress confirmed for July gives carriers a load to plan against, and that is the
case Wesgro needs to hold winter seats (focus area 7). Events used this way sustain "year-round tourism
demand and air connectivity". They also reach beyond the city. The main event can stay in Cape Town
while delegates spend extra nights elsewhere, and Stellenbosch and George can host the next phase of
regional conferences.201

Melbourne's monthly arrivals stay between 7% and 12% of the year, a balance that events and education
travel help create. Yet summer still brings its largest international spike. Victoria builds the balance by
curating a portfolio of global, cultural, regional and business events that generate repeat, predictable
demand spikes across the year. The condition is the shared calendar and a proactive bidding pipeline
behind it. Without them, the gap stays where it is: June to August remains structurally weak for business
events.202

THEME 1.1 · TRAVELLER, BRAND AND PROPOSITION · FOCUS AREA 3


Make the whole province findable and bookable
Outside the Cape Peninsula, the Winelands and the Garden Route, most product cannot be
booked where travellers book, so interest the province has already earned has nowhere to
land.

The challenge
Interest that cannot be booked is lost. Travellers discover and book on platforms, and each growth market
uses its own (section 2.2). China books on Trip.com and Fliggy, India on MakeMyTrip and the Gulf on
Almosafer. Product that is missing from those platforms does not exist for the traveller, however good it is.
For the Western Cape this is the gap between Cape Town and the rest of the province. 203

The gap decides how far growth to 2035 spreads. Cape Town is the gateway, and the case for growth
beyond the Metro rests on visitors moving out and staying longer. The Overberg says it does not package
its offer early enough to be front of mind when travellers book flights. Each room or tour outside the core
that cannot be booked online depends on a traveller willing to pick up the phone.204

The barriers are basic: online booking, card payment and rural broadband. Small operators, community
guides and even large public attractions still sell by phone. Rural broadband sits outside Wesgro's reach
as a national programme.

What the evidence shows

missing: the Overberg and West Coast marine and wildflower assets have no guided tours and no

Wesgro should treat bookability as infrastructure: count it, close it district by district and then place it on

Why it can work
Bookability turns interest the province has already earned into payment. It does not replace awareness:
the Overberg says potential visitors "do not sufficiently understand how close the district is to Cape Town".
Listing and packaging therefore go together. A listing on the platform where a traveller already plans puts
outer-district product in front of that traveller at the moment of decision. The census then shows, district by
district, whether the gap is closing.207

New Zealand packages regional clusters into bookable long-haul itineraries and pairs regional operators
with specialist global travel designers. Its regions gain higher revenue per visitor and better seasonal
resilience. The lesson is to use Cape Town as a gateway that spreads value across the province through
bookable regional experiences. In communities, a booking route also needs a seller behind it. In Santa
Marta in Rio de Janeiro, about 20 resident guides ran the tours by 2015 and kept selling after the launch
programme ended. The condition is connectivity, payment and a seller with a stake. 208

3.1.2 Source market growth and diversification
The second theme is about which markets grow the base. It holds three focus areas: India and China, the
fastest-growing markets, which reach the province through hubs; the mature long-haul markets; and
regional Africa. Volume and value come from different groups, so each needs its own lever (section 2.2).

THEME 1.2 · SOURCE MARKET GROWTH AND DIVERSIFICATION · FOCUS AREA 4


Convert India and China hub traffic
About 101,000 air journeys a year from India and China already reach Cape Town, mainly
through Gulf and African hubs, and with no direct Indian route likely before 2030, converting
that flow is the lever available now.

The opportunity
India and China grow fastest in the base case, at 8% a year to 2035, against 5% for the Middle East and
Brazil in their acceleration group. India and China start from a low base, at about 2.6% and 1.4% of South
Africa's overseas tourists in September 2025. The upside is large, and much of it is already in the air. 209

About 71,000 travellers from India and about 30,000 from China reach Cape Town each year through Gulf
and African hubs. They fly with carriers whose Cape Town business is built on onward traffic. Nine in ten
of Qatar Airways' Cape Town passengers connect through its hub, as do more than 80% of Emirates'.
Hub routes through Dubai, Addis Ababa and Doha run at 65.2% to 72.3% load, so they have room for
more.210

The province is absent where these travellers decide. Both markets plan and book on their own platforms,
where Western Cape product is hard to find (section 2.2). Access and product add friction. Cape Town
has no direct flight to Mumbai or Delhi and thin Indian-friendly adaptation, while Chinese travellers face
complex visas and few Mandarin-language services.211

What the evidence shows

      India and China reach Cape Town only through hubs, and the direct route that would change that is at
      least five years away.

The upside comes in two parts. The hub flow is available now, on routes with spare seats and carriers that
gain from every connecting passenger. The direct route is not: the accelerated case depends on
confirmed direct routes, and without them volume reverts to base. This focus area therefore works the hub
flow now and builds the route case for later.212

A further pool is already in the country. Most of South Africa's visitors from India and from China with Hong
Kong never reach the province, though they hold a visa and a seat (section 2.2). Itineraries sold through
Johannesburg, with a flight to Cape Town on the same ticket, can reach them. 213

The course of action
Wesgro should run India and China as conversion markets through the hubs and as relationship markets
for the direct route. The money shifts toward trade-led conversion rather than broad awareness. India
needs early relationships with its carriers and authorities. China is a long-term investment in which
awareness and indirect volume come before any non-stop service. By November 2025 Cape Town Air
Access had held talks with Cathay Pacific, China Southern, Air India and IndiGo. 214

The hub work comes first because it needs no new seats. Wesgro agrees joint market support with the
three hub carriers in 2027, and activation on the Chinese and Indian platforms runs in market from 2028
(section 4.3). The direct route cases wait for runway capacity and go to carriers from 2032. Wesgro's plan
targets three international tourism marketing campaigns over FY26 to FY30. Any that run in India or China
should convert inside the platforms and with the hub carriers.215

point of decision, which broad awareness does not.

THEME 1.2 · SOURCE MARKET GROWTH AND DIVERSIFICATION · FOCUS AREA 5


Increase yield in mature markets
The mature markets already pay the most and fill the fullest routes, so growth there comes
from more value per visitor and more seats on existing routes, not from broad awareness
campaigns.

The opportunity
The mature markets are where the province earns its premium. High-spend visitors come from Europe
and North America, "tying future value growth to slower-growing source markets". The mature group
grows steadily to 2035, but more slowly than the hub and acceleration markets (section 2.2). Growth here
comes from value per visitor more than from volume.217

Value matters to the growth case because seats are scarce. Five mature long-haul routes already run
above 80% load: Newark, Atlanta, Frankfurt, Heathrow and Washington. The runway binds at the peak
until relief arrives in about 2033 (section 2.3). Each extra rand per visitor on a full route is growth that
needs no extra seat. Longer stays and travel beyond the Metro add value in the same way.218

Two things stand in the way: carrier pricing and the province's own measures. On full routes, carriers are
expected to raise fares rather than add new services, which "will frustrate that desire for increased
visitors". The province must therefore earn more per visitor without losing share. Yet its targets count
arrivals, and spend per visitor by market is printed for a single year.219

The constraint is well documented; the lever is not. The five routes are full and their visitors pay most. Yet
none of the work behind this report holds fare, revenue-per-seat or yield data. None models a yield uplift
either. The yield lever rests on how carriers are expected to behave on full routes. This focus area
therefore sets yield as an indicator to establish, not a target to hit, until spend per visitor by market is
tracked every year [yield baseline: SAT spend per visitor by market as an annual series; only 2024 is
printed].

Market share can be measured now. Over 2022 to 2024 the province took under 0.3% of air outbound
trips from each of the UK, the USA, Germany and France (section 2.2). Of the three largest markets, only
the USA gained share of Cape Town arrivals from 2019 to 2024, rising 3.8 points to 15.0%. 220

The course of action
Wesgro should protect share and raise value per visitor in the mature markets, and work the full routes for
frequency and gauge before it chases new ones. Drawing these visitors beyond the Metro serves the
same end while the growth markets develop. There is tactical room for added frequency on some routes
and larger aircraft from some airlines.221

The full routes come first. From 2027 Wesgro makes the frequency and gauge case to their carriers,
because seats held now protect share until the runway relief arrives (section 4.3). Luxury-advisor
distribution follows in 2028. Spend per visitor and length of stay join Wesgro's own targets from 2029,
once the data-sharing protocol supplies them (focus area 18).

Why it can work
Yield work suits full routes. When seats are scarce, a visitor who stays longer, travels further and spends
more adds value without adding a seat. Selling to the highest-spending segment through luxury advisors
lifts the average spend of the travellers the seats already carry. Full routes also give Wesgro a frequency
case to put, though not a promise of seats, because carriers are expected to raise fares first. 222

Kenya shows that value per visitor can outrun volume. With about 2.4 million arrivals in 2024, against
Morocco's 17.4 million, Kenya earned about US$1,460 per international arrival. Morocco earned about
US$630 and the Western Cape about US$890. The condition is a measure. Without spend per visitor by
market as an annual series, yield can be neither managed nor judged, so the data-sharing protocol comes
first (focus area 18).223

THEME 1.2 · SOURCE MARKET GROWTH AND DIVERSIFICATION · FOCUS AREA 6


Build a regional foundation (Africa)
Regional Africa is the province's steadiest volume and its least developed premium market:
Zimbabwe and Namibia supply 62% of African arrivals, while West African visitors spend the
most per visit.

The opportunity
Africa is the province's nearest market and its steadiest. Namibia and Zimbabwe "add stability, repeat
demand and counter-seasonal volume". Regional trips are shorter in lead time and easier to convert,
which helps the green season. Africa is therefore both a base to keep and a market to grow (section
2.2).224

The opportunity to 2035 is premium growth on top of that base. Nigerian and Ghanaian visitors spend as
much per visit as visitors from the mature markets, from a far smaller base (section 2.2). Kenya's case
rests on scale rather than spend. Its visitors spend R16,900 a visit, but its pool of 240,000 to 400,000 trips
a year is second only to Nigeria's outside South Africa. The growth model also gives it the strongest
regional upside. Cape Town is not a cold start in Nigeria or Kenya, where awareness of South Africa
exists and needs converting rather than building from scratch.225

Access is the constraint. Routes to Luanda, Maputo, Gaborone, Harare and Mauritius run above 80%
load, so added demand there meets full aircraft. Visas add friction. South Africa ranked 33rd of 54 African
countries on the Africa Visa Openness Index, as BDO reported in 2024. The CTICC also reports that
regional African event demand is constrained by visa requirements.226

Africa holds two different markets. A Southern African base delivers volume, while Nigeria and Kenya offer
premium demand from a large addressable pool. Three cautions apply. Nigeria and Ghana have no row in
the growth model, so no 2035 arrivals figure exists for the two highest-spending African markets [Nigeria
and Ghana arrivals targets: not modelled; Wesgro Research to add both]. Ghana sits in the watch tier for
brand seeding rather than conversion. And regional travellers tend to stop in Johannesburg rather than
continue to Cape Town.227

The course of action
Wesgro should run Africa as two markets: a regional volume base held through capacity and value, and a
premium growth market in Nigeria and Kenya won through conversion. Nigeria is the priority for luxury,
status-driven and conference travel. Kenya is the priority for business mobility and premium urban breaks,
and both are reached through premium activation and the trade.228

The groundwork comes first. In 2027 Nigeria and Ghana enter the growth model, because the two
highest-spending African markets have no targets yet (section 4.3). Premium activation in Nigeria and
Kenya starts in 2028. The capacity case on the five full regional routes follows from 2029, made by Air
Access once the route fund is running (focus area 7).

Why it can work
The two-market approach matches the lever to the demand. On the five full regional routes, volume is
capped by seats, so the capacity case is where it grows. Premium demand in Nigeria and Kenya already
knows South Africa, which ranks second only to Dubai in Kenyan outbound consideration. Activation
converts that awareness rather than having to create it. Conferences with leisure extensions serve both,
because business travel can help smooth demand across the calendar and event attendance can become
longer stays.229

Kenya splits its demand in a similar way. Regional African markets drive its volume and business travel,
while long-haul markets bring leisure demand and higher spend per visitor. It draws 41% of its arrivals
from Africa, mainly from Uganda and Tanzania. Nairobi serves as East Africa's aviation, diplomatic and
business hub through Kenya Airways' regional network. Its arrivals grew 14.6% in 2024 to about 2.4
million, against world growth of 11%. The transferable changes are to treat MICE as a system lever and to
align city, provincial and national actors around one gateway strategy. The condition is seats and visas.
Activation in Nigeria and Kenya converts only travellers who can get a visa and a seat. 230

3.2 Pillar 2: Transform supply

Supply sets the ceiling on the growth case. The airport caps arrivals before its relief arrives,
and outside the Metro the product exists but is hard to reach, book or stay in.

Pillar 2 removes the mobility, infrastructure and product constraints that limit how many visitors the
province can carry and where they can go. Demand work in Pillar 1 fills seats only if the seats exist. It
pays outside the Metro only if visitors can reach, book and stay in the product there (section 2.3).

The first constraint is the gateway. The growth model draws current air access as a line at 1.8 million
inbound seats, and the base case crosses it at the 2030 year point. The runway binds at peak hours
before that. The international terminal upgrade at Cape Town International is due about 2032 and the
relocated runway about 2033.231

Transport is a connectivity and dispersal challenge, not a province-wide capacity crisis. Cape Town
International is the key gateway risk. Beyond the gateway, the constraint is reach and readiness.
Accessibility to attractions and services falls from 4.7 out of 5 in the Cape Metro to 1.0 in the Central
Karoo. Only one touring route of six is rated Ready.232

The pillar holds two themes. The first is how visitors reach the province and move through it (focus areas
7 to 9). The second is what they find on arrival (focus areas 10 to 12).

Only the air entry needs more transport capacity. Cruise has room at the berth: it handled 83 calls in
2024/25 against a season capacity of 150 or more. The flagship nature sites face a limit of a different kind,
which calls for managing flows rather than adding volume (focus area 12). The other three entries concern
reach, product and experience. The entries also have an order. Seats into the gateway come first;
corridors, beds and booking then decide where growth lands (section 2.3). Visas, air service agreements
and the runway are national levers, which the province leads by escalation in Pillar 3 (section 3.3.2). 233

By 2035 the pillar must do two things. It must hold and add seats on the full routes until runway relief
arrives. It must also lift the share of arrivals and spend outside the Metro, by giving visitors product they
can reach, book and stay in. Initiatives I6 to I11 deliver the six entries (section 4.3).

3.2.1 Accessibility, connectivity and infrastructure
The first supply theme covers how visitors reach the province and move through it. Air access and cruise
bring visitors in, and internal mobility carries them beyond Cape Town.

THEME 2.1 · ACCESSIBILITY, CONNECTIVITY AND INFRASTRUCTURE · FOCUS AREA 7


Win direct routes and add seats on full routes
Eleven of 27 international routes run above 80% load and carriers are expected to price
before adding seats, so the province must win frequency and larger aircraft before runway
relief in about 2033.

The challenge
Cape Town International is the province's only international airport, so the growth case runs through one
gateway. On the growth model, seats run out before demand does, at the 2030 year point. Growth to 2035
is therefore a supply programme before it is a marketing one (section 2.3).234

Two limits bind before that line. The first is commercial. Cape Town's best routes already run full, and on
the mature long-haul routes carriers are expected to raise fares rather than add services. The second is
physical. A single main runway caps movements at about 30 an hour, and the relocated runway that lifts
the cap is due about 2033. ACSA's latest schedule is earlier, with the runway finished by December 2029,
subject to contract award. The phasing in Chapter 4 assumes about 2033 and treats the earlier date as
upside.235

Yet the province starts from strength. It added 17 mainline international routes between 2015 and 2025,
more than any of eight peer airports. The task is to hold and enlarge those routes until the runway arrives,
and to have the next ones ready when it does.236

The evidence describes a timing problem more than a demand problem. The fares forecast covers the
mature long-haul routes only, and no fare data yet tests it. The five regional markets that also run above
80% are named for more capacity. The hub routes still have room, with Dubai at 72.3%, Addis Ababa at
71.9% and Doha at 65.2%. On those routes marketing can still fill seats.237

Relief also comes in the wrong order: "the certain fix is the latest, and the earliest big-capacity option is
the least certain". George doubles its capacity but flies domestic routes only, and Cape Winelands has no
firm named carrier. The province's aviation strategy identifies a possible upgrade that would let George
handle international traffic. No airline is among the 16 organisations whose views this report draws on, so
carriers' views on frequency and gauge are untested.238

The course of action
The approach splits the air agenda by horizon. Until runway relief arrives in about 2033, the first job is to
fill and enlarge what already flies. The near-term moves are added frequency from Emirates, Qatar
Airways and Ethiopian Airlines, with airline incentives coordinated through Cape Town Air Access. Route
cases for India and China are the long-term move. Wesgro also presses for more seats to Luanda,
Maputo, Gaborone, Harare and Mauritius, the five regional markets above 80%.239

Wesgro's plan also expects 10 new routes, first-time scheduled international services, by FY30. That is
two a year. Cape Town Air Access counts 33 route launches since 2015, about three a year; the 17
mainline routes added over the same decade make about 1.7 a year (rates computed). On the plan's own
definition the target sits below the partnership's recent pace. Filling existing seats first still leaves room for
them, because the runway binds at peak hours and new routes can land outside them. As reported in
November 2025, Cape Town Air Access's short to medium term targets spanned Africa, Saudi Arabia,
China, Hong Kong, India and Australia. The African and Saudi links fit before runway relief. 240

Cape Town Air Access's route fund becomes a ring-fenced public-private fund, designed in 2027 and
2028. It is then running when the regional frequency cases are made from 2029. The India and China
cases go to carriers from 2032, as runway capacity arrives (section 4.3). ACSA delivers the runway and
terminals, and Wesgro tracks them quarterly as the first risk. Air service agreements, the cap on US
carriers and immigration processing sit with national government, so the province escalates them (section
3.3.2).
