Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                   85
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035                                          DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




Why it can work
The programme works on the airline's decision, not the visitor's. A carrier on a mature long-haul route
seeks revenue through fares, yet there is tactical room for added frequency and larger aircraft. Joint
marketing and a ring-fenced fund lower the carrier's risk on extra capacity. The province then buys seats,
not higher fares. Belly cargo strengthens the case. Cape Town International is the province's only airport
for international air freight, all of it carried in passenger aircraft. Exports made up 67% of its air freight in
2023. New Zealand tied marketing budgets to long-haul corridors and designed campaigns around airline
breakeven. It reports more than 16,000 incremental bookings and a return of about NZ$55 million in
2023/24.241

Chile shows the infrastructure half. A private concession funded about US$900 million of expansion at
Santiago, and international seats grew about 40% to 50% between 2013 and 2019 under open-skies
agreements. The route fund is the province's to scale; open skies and private airport finance are national
levers, carried in the escalation agenda (focus area 19). The condition is the runway. If relief slips past
about 2033, existing routes must carry growth for longer.242

THEME 2.1 · ACCESSIBILITY, CONNECTIVITY AND INFRASTRUCTURE · FOCUS AREA 8


Grow cruise calls and ports
Cruise added about R1.79bn to provincial GDP-R in 2024/25, and Cape Town's berth took
83 calls against room for 150 or more, so growth comes from ships, excursions and day
stops, not port capital.

The opportunity
Cruise has room to grow at the berth Cape Town already has. The equipped berth handled 83 calls in
2024/25 against a season capacity of 150 or more. That season brought about R1.99bn of spending
before displacement and added about R1.79bn to provincial GDP-R after it. Growth here needs product
and marketing, not capital works.243

International passengers drive the value (section 2.3). World cruises made up 42 of the 79 vessel visits,
and their passengers added R802m to provincial GDP-R, 73% of what all passengers added. The season
also earned R1.326bn of net foreign exchange and R259m in taxes.244

Cruise also carries spend beyond the city. Cape Town anchors the itinerary, and Mossel Bay and
Saldanha Bay can extend it east and west. Stakeholders name home porting and expedition vessels as
the segments that bring longer stays and higher local spend.245

What limits growth is ships and product, not berths (section 2.3). The equipped berth takes ships of up to
300m, the South-Easter delays berthing and there is no shore power. Port capital goes to freight:
Saldanha's R4bn iron-ore expansion and Cape Town's container expansion add no cruise capacity.246

StratEcon counts 79 vessel visits and 127,128 passengers, the Diagnostic 79 and 127,178, and the Infrastructure Report 83 calls. Mossel
Bay's 27 a season is its own figure; section 2.3 sets out the Mossel Bay counts.

The evidence sets a clear stance: protect Cape Town, back Mossel Bay now and watch Saldanha Bay.
Mossel Bay handled 21 calls in 2024/25, opened a new passenger facility in November 2025 and expects
about 23 calls next season. Yet ships anchor and tender there, and only small and mid-sized ships call.
Saldanha Bay has no passenger facility. The regional ports work as day stops rather than home ports, so
they spread spend rather than multiply the passenger total.247

Wesgro's plan sets the target: 250 ship calls over FY26 to FY30, about 50 a year. The plan rounds up an
annual average of 47 calls and notes that 20 of the 67 calls in 2023/24 were ships rerouted by the Middle
East conflict. At about 50 a year the target sits below the 79 vessel visits of 2024/25. It is a floor the berth
already clears, not a case for a second berth.248

The course of action
The approach works with the berth Cape Town has rather than waiting for a new one. Cruise Cape Town
leads, and the sequence follows readiness. Line targeting, the Mossel Bay excursion packages and one
definition of a call for reporting against the 250 are designed in 2027. Targeting starts with world-cruise
lines whose ships fit the 300m berth. Marketing to expedition and home-porting operators follows in 2028,
alongside community product on cruise-operator channels (section 4.3). Saldanha Bay waits until demand
is proven, because its port capital serves iron ore.

The port itself stays with its owners. Transnet National Ports Authority and the V&A Waterfront hold berth
scheduling, wind delays, shore power and the 300m limit. The national ask is that Transnet puts cruise
into port planning, since current Western Cape port capital carries none.

 Who acts              Action                                                                                 Lead and partners

                       300m berth and to expedition and home-porting operators, with pre-                     Destination Marketing; City of

                       carries no cruise capacity



Why it can work
The actions match the constraint. The berth has headroom; the gaps are ship-size flexibility, weather
reliability and tourism product. Each line won with ships that fit adds calls without capital works. Packaged
excursions give passengers more to buy ashore, and regional day stops spread that spend along the
coast.249

The best precedent is local. On its own count, Mossel Bay's calls rose from four before COVID to 27 a
season, supported by closer coordination and better excursion packaging. The town credits its partnership
with Wesgro. No benchmark destination offers a cruise precedent, so the case rests on Mossel Bay. The
condition is that today's ship deployments hold. Recent growth leans on Red Sea reroutes, and their
reversal is the main risk.250

THEME 2.1 · ACCESSIBILITY, CONNECTIVITY AND INFRASTRUCTURE · FOCUS AREA 9


Accelerate tourism-enabling internal mobility
Accessibility falls from 4.7 out of 5 in the Cape Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo, and
beyond the Metro and the Garden Route the province is effectively car-only.

The challenge
Growth to 2035 has to spread beyond Cape Town, yet large parts of the province outside the Metro and
the Garden Route are effectively car-only. The N2 is the only continuous inter-regional tourism corridor, a
single point of failure. Limited public transport "restricts access for non-driving visitors and reduces the
addressable tourism market". Section 2.3 shows readiness and access falling with each step away from
the Metro.251

The problem is reach, not capacity. Even in the Metro no rail line reaches the airport, and safety
perceptions and limited visitor wayfinding hold back Metrorail's tourism potential. In April 2025 the
Department of Mobility reported that no mass public transport served the airport and that the MyCiTi
airport service had been suspended since 2022. Stakeholders call weak public transport, congestion and
unreliable last-mile access structural barriers to visitor movement.252

No single body owns the visitor's journey. The Department of Mobility notes that mobility responsibilities
are split across three tiers of government, and argues the case for a dedicated transport authority. 253

What the evidence shows

The evidence shows transport assets that exist but are not run for visitors. The Cape Town to Simon's

Simon's Town as a ready-made, low-cost scenic corridor. Yet its visitor branding dates to 2007. The gaps
between towns are services rather than roads. The Garden Route has no inter-town shuttle despite

obvious demand, and Hermanus, Stanford, Elim and Agulhas have no public tourism transport between

The shuttle gap has a commercial fix. A shared shuttle needs demand aggregation and booking
integration to work, as !Khwa ttu says of the West Coast. Part of the answer is therefore within reach of a
marketing and packaging body, while the rail and road assets stay with their owners.255

The course of action
Wesgro does not build transport. It makes existing transport usable for visitors and gathers the demand
that makes shuttles pay. The Department of Mobility leads the work, with Wesgro's Tourism unit. The
Southern Line product, shuttle aggregation and transfers sold inside itineraries are piloted in 2027 and
2028. Shuttle products run from 2029, as the City and PRASA complete the Central Line recovery and
MyCiTi Phase 2. The April 2025 draft land transport framework targets MyCiTi Phase 2A to Khayelitsha
and Mitchells Plain for 2027, which brings township product within reach sooner. The Cape Town to
George rapid rail decision and the last mile to Cape Point come later in the period (section 4.3). National
government is asked to lift the rule that bars bank-card payments on the George bus and to fund
passenger rail beyond the Metro. The Department of Mobility's draft Tourism Transport Strategy adds the
airport leg and touring-route signage, so Wesgro also sells airport transfers inside itineraries.256

Why it can work
The mechanism is aggregation. A shuttle pays only at volume, and pooled bookings across operators,
sold inside a multi-day itinerary, turn scattered requests into a load that can run. The Southern Line needs
no new track to become a product, only branding, passes and marketing. Each step widens the market to
visitors without a car, the market that limited public transport now narrows. 257

None of the six benchmark destinations carries an internal mobility lever with figures. Medellín shows the
principle at city scale. A cable-car line carrying about 35,000 passengers a day and six outdoor escalators,
built for Comuna 13's residents, became part of the visit. The area drew 1.98 million visitors in 2025, 52%
of them international. The Cape Winelands shows it at home: its heritage tram, built to connect wine
estates, solves the drink-and-drive barrier. The condition is shared ownership and an early capacity limit.

3.2.2 Product and destination capacity
The second supply theme covers what visitors find once they arrive: product beyond the icons, the quality
of the stay and the limits of the flagship sites. The three focus areas work as a set. New product gives
visitors a reason to travel outward, quality standards make that product safe to sell and flow management
at the flagships sends the overflow to it.

THEME 2.2 · PRODUCT AND DESTINATION CAPACITY · FOCUS AREA 10


Support diversified route and product development
The province does not lack attractions; it lacks finished experiences around them, with
assets outside the Metro left without guides, interpretation or bookable product and one
touring route of six rated Ready.

The challenge
The growth case needs visitors to travel beyond the Cape Peninsula, stay longer and spend in more
places. The province has the assets to support that. Of 50,332 mapped tourism assets, the Cape Metro
holds 40.3% and the five other districts the rest. The West Coast has the highest natural-asset
attractiveness in the province and the Central Karoo its most unique niche assets.259

Yet both score lowest on readiness, and the product around the assets is unfinished (section 2.3). Eleven
of 16 organisations engaged see the offer as concentrated around Cape Town and a narrow set of icons.
The Department of Economic Development adds that destination development has focused more on
managing barriers than on building product outside the Cape Peninsula.260

Nine of the ten places international tourists visit most are in the Metro. That concentration costs the
province twice. The flagships run full in season while the township attractions are underused. Further out,
strong product sits where visitors cannot easily reach or afford it.261

What the evidence shows

      Assets are spread across the province, but product, packaging and investment stop at the core routes.

The evidence locates the constraint. It is less about creating new attractions and more about turning
existing assets into complete, accessible visitor experiences. Each route falls short on something different:
accommodation and bookability on the West Coast, bookability on Route 62 and depth beyond Hermanus
on the Whale Coast. The Winelands and the Garden Route fall short only on safety (section 2.3). 262

No committed capital yet follows the gap. No source counts the assets that have no product around them
[assets with no bookable product or guided access, by district: needs the Wesgro supply database behind
the 50,332 count]. The first design task is therefore to size the gap district by district.263

The course of action
Wesgro works outward from strength. The Garden Route, the Cape Winelands and the Overberg already
have strong underlying product and need connectivity, packaging and service depth. Route 62 and the
West Coast are packaged as slow-travel products. In 2027 and 2028 the Tourism unit designs bookable
itineraries for all six touring routes. Delivery starts from 2029 on the Garden Route, the Cape Winelands
and the Overberg, which are closest to ready. It moves to Route 62 and the West Coast once their product
is online (focus area 3; section 4.3).264

The City is a natural partner. Its framework for 2019 to 2024 proposed a Cape Town+ offer linking the city
with the surrounding regions. Stellenbosch and George can anchor regional business events, which gives
the Convention Bureau a part to play. The capital sits with others. Accommodation investors and
municipalities add beds and service depth in secondary towns later in the period (focus area 15).
SANParks carries the precinct projects, and rural broadband sits with national government (focus area
3).265

The actions supply what the assets lack: a guide, a bed and a booking route. Packaging turns scattered

Overberg shows both the pull and the limit. Its whale-watching anchor sustains operator confidence
despite flat accessibility, yet De Hoop still lacks accommodation investment and year-round product is
unfunded.266

THEME 2.2 · PRODUCT AND DESTINATION CAPACITY · FOCUS AREA 11


Align visitor experience in-destination
The province offers luxury estates and basic self-catering with no mid-scale branded tier
between them, and service, interpretation and language readiness thin out with distance
from Cape Town.

The challenge
The growth case needs visitors the current offer serves poorly. Province-wide, no midscale branded
product sits between luxury estates and basic self-catering. In the Winelands, a luxury-skewed product
mix excludes mid-market and family segments. The Garden Route is short of quality-graded hotels that
anchor midweek, conference or business travel.268

The experience is weakest at the arrival gate and at the periphery. The international airport is the most
negative node in Cape Town's online mentions. Further out, readiness falls with distance from the Metro
(section 2.3). Accommodation readiness drops from 4.7 in the Metro to 2.0 in the Central Karoo. 269

The growth markets add their own needs. Chinese visitors find few Mandarin-language services. Indian
visitors see little sign of vegetarian options or Indian-language guides. Gulf visitors find no Arabic-
language booking or local-currency options. These are the markets Pillar 1 courts, so the gaps sit on the
growth path (section 3.1). Halal readiness belongs on the same list, since the province's Tourism Blueprint
names halal tourism among the latent forms whose potential return has not yet been reached. 270

The evidence shows a gap in the middle of the market and at the service counter, not a shortage of
premium product. Service quality worries the operators themselves: CapeNature is cautious about scaling
international demand while service readiness is uneven across its sites.271

Two measures are missing. No source counts mid-scale rooms or beds by district [mid-scale room count
by district: needs the Wesgro supply database or an accommodation audit], and none prints a visitor
satisfaction score.

The course of action
The quality charter comes first, because it sets the standard the other actions aim at. DEDAT leads it with
Wesgro's Tourism unit. It is a province-wide framework that aligns all tourism stakeholders under
consistent benchmarks. It is phased by district, with certification visible on booking platforms and support
for SMMEs to comply. Design runs through 2027 and 2028, and the district phases start in 2029 (section
4.3).272

The investment case for mid-scale hotels follows the charter from 2029, so that investors build to a known
standard (focus area 15). The arrival gate sits outside the province's reach: Home Affairs and ACSA hold
immigration processing and peak-hour throughput at Cape Town International, so the province presses
them.

Why it can work
A visible standard solves a problem of trust. A visitor booking a guesthouse on Route 62 cannot judge its
service from abroad; certification shown on the booking platform can. The same standard gives small
operators a clear target and a route to support, and it lets the province sell product outside the core with
confidence.

Quality governance was Valencia's primary lever. Its Qualitur programme and SICTED quality system
raised service consistency, reputation and repeat visitation. The city received 3.5 million international
visitors in 2024, 13.9% more than in 2023 and 24.4% above 2019. Singapore's tourism board used a hotel
industry transformation map, a hotel rejuvenation fund and the Hotels Act. The aim was to move from
short stopovers towards longer stays and higher spend. The condition is that small businesses can
comply. The compliance burden and skills shortages are the main risks, so the charter needs SMME
support from the start and the skills work in focus area 20.273

THEME 2.2 · PRODUCT AND DESTINATION CAPACITY · FOCUS AREA 12


Balance impact on nature-based attractions
Boulders is near carrying capacity and Cableway queues reach three hours, so growth at
the flagship nature sites must come from managing flows and steering visitors to under-
marketed reserves.

The challenge
The flagship nature sites carry the province's brand. Table Mountain, Robben Island and Kirstenbosch
form the only globally branded landmark cluster, and the Metro holds five of the province's six highest-
volume attractions. In season they run full (section 2.3). Peak queues at the Cableway of up to three hours
detract from the visitor experience.274

More volume at these sites is the wrong goal. The Department of Economic Development wants further
growth directed to under-visited parts of the province rather than constrained flagships. Yet the flagships
congest at the peak while the overflow reserves remain under-marketed. This report names Stony Point, a
penguin colony in the Overberg, as the alternative to Boulders at the peak. Its visitors rose 28% over the
festive season (section 2.3).275

Nature tourism also funds conservation. CapeNature cannot rely on public funding alone and treats
tourism as a revenue business to support its conservation mandate. Flooding, fire and heat are already
closing CapeNature sites, so the reserves that should take the overflow are themselves under strain. 276

Why it can work
Flow management protects the visit and the revenue together. Digital flow tools and tiered pricing spread
arrivals across the day and the season. A site then serves its visitors without a three-hour queue and
earns more at the peak. Marketing the alternatives at the point of booking sends the overflow to the under-
marketed reserves, and their income pays for conservation there.

New Zealand uses land-use controls and premium curation on the principle that capacity discipline
protects long-term pricing power. Kenya treats conservation as national economic infrastructure, with
tourism-funded conservation financing and community conservancies that sustain demand and
environmental integrity. The City already runs the model at small scale. Since August 2025 Witzands
permits have been capped by day, and 80% are pre-booked online. The condition is the operators. They
must share their counts, and their mandates must allow pricing: CapeNature's statutory affordability
mandate has kept its prices conservative, and CapeNature is now moving towards tiered pricing.280

3.3 Pillar 3: Build enabling capability

Pillar 3 decides whether the other two can be delivered and shared: who benefits, who pays,
who decides and who measures.

Pillar 3 sets out to build an inclusive, well-governed tourism system that supports sustainable growth,
returns and development. The other two pillars depend on it. Demand work fills seats and supply work
opens the regions, but neither lasts unless someone owns the decisions, pays for the assets and counts
the results. Section 2.4 found that system thin at each point the action plan relies on.

Each question starts from a gap. On who benefits, 20 township and rural communities hold 18.4% of the
province's residents but receive a traced 0.02% to 0.04% of foreign direct spend. On who pays, the capital
pipeline funds airports, ports, roads, rail and the Metro waterfront, but almost none of it reaches regional
product, bookability or community assets. On who decides, visas and air service agreements sit with
national government, and mobility mandates are split across three tiers. On who measures, no approved
methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs.281

Two themes and eight focus areas carry the pillar. The first theme, investment, inclusion and
sustainability, settles who benefits from growth, how it is paid for and how it lasts. It gives community
product a local seller, manages climate risk through funded local bodies and builds an investment pipeline
that follows demand (focus areas 13 to 15). The second theme, governance and leadership, settles who
decides and who measures. It aligns the three tiers of government, turns industry forums into one joint
plan and agrees how data is shared (focus areas 16 to 18). The same theme holds two further focus
areas. Focus area 19 takes the national deficits in infrastructure, visas and safety to the tier that holds
them. Focus area 20 commissions the labour analysis a skills plan will rest on.

The eight entries work as a sequence, not a menu. Governance starts alongside the first seat and
bookability moves (section 3.4). Steerco, the data-sharing protocol and the escalation agenda start in the
first quarter of 2027, because every other initiative reports through them (section 4.2). The community
pilots start in the same phase, with booking routes shared with focus area 3 and safety plans drawn from
focus area 19. Climate risk, local tourism funding, the investment pipeline and the labour analysis spend
2027 and 2028 on design. Each first needs a baseline or a case that does not yet exist. Capital for product
and community assets comes last. It follows demand that the pilots and the regional product have shown,
not the reverse (section 2.1).

The evidence behind the pillar is mostly qualitative. It rests on statements attributed to the 16
organisations with recorded insights, a one-page table of what each tier holds, the community profiles and
the district gap tables. The pillar therefore carries more open items than the other two. Several entries
start by building the baseline they will be judged against. Provincial and City documents narrow some of
these gaps and offer programmes to build on; each entry cites the ones it uses.282

By 2035 the pillar must leave the province able to deliver its growth and share it. Visitor spend should
reach the communities, measured against a reference point of 1% of foreign direct spend, R259m a year
at FY2025 levels (section 5.3). Local tourism bodies should hold stable funding, and tourism projects
should reach financial close. The national tier should answer the province's asks, and the province should
count its own tourism jobs and spend. Initiatives I12 to I18 carry the eight focus areas, with focus areas 16
and 17 sharing one forum in I15 (section 4.1).

3.3.1 Investment, inclusion and sustainability
The first enabling theme covers who benefits from growth, how it is paid for and how it lasts. It holds three
focus areas: community inclusion, climate risk and investment. The inclusion model is set out at concept
level, and Chapter 4 assigns its roles and funding.

THEME 3.1 · INVESTMENT, INCLUSION AND SUSTAINABILITY · FOCUS AREA 13


Embed local destination bodies for community inclusion
Twenty communities hold 18.4% of the province's residents but receive only a small
documented share of foreign direct spend, and in eleven the binding constraint is
organisation, not distance or product.

The opportunity
Community inclusion decides whether growth to 2035 is shared. Inclusion here means visitor activity
inside the community: a tour, a meal, a bed or a market. A resident commuting to a hotel job in town
counts for little or nothing. On that measure the province starts near zero. Foreign visitors spent R25.9bn
in FY2025, and the 20 shortlisted communities received a documented floor of R4m to R10m of it. 283

The prize is large. At 1% of FY2025 foreign direct spend, the 20 communities would receive R259m a
year; at 1% of the 2035 spend ambition, R547m. Both are reference points, not adopted targets (section
5.3). The visitors are already close, and organisation rather than distance keeps their spend out (section
2.4).284

Inclusion also widens what the province sells. SATVITO finds that more township and village product
could widen market reach and lift visitor spend, yet negative safety perceptions of the townships hold
demand back. The opportunity opens only to a community that can sell and a visit that is safe. 285

Source: SME and Community Inclusion Plan, Sep 2026 (SCIP; desk research 17 to 23 September 2026, ratings from the community side with
LTO confirmation outstanding). The R259m is 1% of the R25.9bn FY2025 foreign direct spend in the Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p11.
The plan does not describe how the spend floor was traced. The multiple of today's floor is computed.

The evidence places the main gap in selling, not proximity. Organisation binds in 11 of the 20
communities, attraction in six and distance in none. No profiled community yet rates Ready on digital and
bookability. The spend floor is uncertain, because the method behind it is not yet shown. [Open item: the
Inclusion Plan to set out how the R4m to R10m floor was traced.]286

The cases set the order of action: a seller with a stake, then a safe and workable visit, then product that
keeps spend in the community and only then exposure. Kliptown in Soweto shows the cost of the reverse
order. Walter Sisulu Square cost R160 million to R180 million, out of the R436 million first allocated to
Kliptown, before an operator was in place (section 2.1). By 2017 it drew about 4,000 museum visitors a
year, against a business case of almost 200,000 international visitors. Safety is the second gate. In Rio,
security collapsed in 2017 and public support was withdrawn early. Nine communities record a safety-
perception gap and five record extortion, so every pilot needs a site-specific safety plan before launch. 287

The course of action
Wesgro should connect community product to the demand it already moves, and leave the building of
selling bodies to municipalities, LTOs and the communities themselves. The model has three parts: a
selling capability that works, a link to demand that already exists and gates that must be passed before
activation and investment. The four gates are a site-specific safety and access plan, a viable operating
model, an operating budget and a maintenance and marketing covenant.288

In 2027 Wesgro and its partners choose two or three pilots on readiness and agree who funds a seller
with a stake in each. Safety plans follow in the same year (section 4.3). In 2028 each pilot opens one live
booking route and runs a fixed-date test, and Wesgro places the product in its itineraries and calendars.
Investment follows demand, and pilots scale from 2029 only once bookings, local spend, delivery and
safety meet thresholds agreed before launch. In Metro pilots, the Cultures of Cape Town programme and
local tourism forums can host the seller, if they run. The City's framework proposed both, with a special
focus on the Cape Flats and townships.289

Why it can work
The model supplies the missing piece and little else. Demand already passes within minutes of most
communities. A local seller with a booking route turns that passing demand into bookings, and the gates

Resident sellers have outlasted the programmes that launched them. In Rio de Janeiro's Santa Marta,
about 20 resident guides ran the tours by 2015 and outside jeep firms had left. Medellín's Comuna 13
drew 1.98 million visitors in 2025 around resident-run product. On Vilakazi Street in Soweto, residents
opened Sakhumzi in 2001 with four employees; it employed about 110 by 2019. The condition is a seller
that survives without a sponsor. Costa Rica's community network turned over more than USD 800,000 in
2013 and did not survive donor exit, dissolving at the end of 2018, though the record names no single
cause. The lesson drawn there is an entity with its own selling channel and a succession plan that does
not rest on a donor. Most first moves carry no budget line, and the LTOs that would carry a seller depend
on municipal funding that Circular 131 has disrupted (focus area 14). No pilot launches until a named
municipality or partner has funded its seller.291

THEME 3.1 · INVESTMENT, INCLUSION AND SUSTAINABILITY · FOCUS AREA 14


Manage climate risk and sustainable tourism
Climate is already costing the province sites, routes and revenue, while the local tourism
bodies that would manage the response depend on municipal funding that MFMA Circular
No. 131 has disrupted.

The challenge
Growth to 2035 rests on sites and routes that weather can close, and the losses already fall on the
flagship sites (section 2.4). Each district carries its own climate risk, from water security on the Garden
Route to extreme summer heat in the Central Karoo. Water scarcity acts as a hard ceiling on visitor load in
high-growth nodes such as the Garden Route. A risk spread across every district needs an owner in each.
The risk weighs most where tourism weighs most. In 2023 tourist spend equalled 15.6% of GDPR in the
Overberg and 16.8% on the West Coast, against 6.7% in the Metro. The Garden Route and Central Karoo
reviews print identical series, which the Treasury is to confirm (section 5.4).292

Yet the bodies that should manage the response locally are the weakest link. The Department of
Economic Development rates Western Cape and Cape Town funding for destination management and
marketing materially below peer destinations. It also finds that most local tourism organisations (LTOs)
cannot sustain operations without municipal funding, which MFMA Circular No. 131 has disrupted. A body
unsure of next year's budget cannot plan for a flood or a fire season.293

The evidence is dated and site-specific, but it does not add up to a provincial picture. No source puts a
provincial figure on tourism revenue at risk from weather or water [provincial estimate of tourism revenue
at risk: none in hand]. None counts the LTOs or their grant funding [number of Western Cape LTOs and
their municipal funding before and after Circular 131: DEDAT to supply]. The LTO funding finding rests on
two organisations, the Department of Economic Development and the West Coast District Municipality. 294

The response exists in pieces. Mossel Bay's membership inspections check water-wise and green
practices, the only working sustainability standard on record. CapeNature is reassessing asset formats
and siting to cut losses from flooding, fire and extreme weather. What is missing is a provincial record of
losses and a stable owner for local destination management.295

The course of action
Wesgro should treat climate as a destination-management risk it records and plans around, and press for
stable funding of the local bodies that do the work. In 2027 and 2028 DEDAT and Wesgro's Research unit
build a climate and weather risk register for sites and routes, starting with the dated losses (section 4.3).
They also design an LTO funding model that answers Circular 131 and tests the Blueprint's district model
(focus area 16). Wesgro routes itineraries around Meiringspoort while it is closed. From 2029 funding
agreements with a development mandate are signed, and routing around closures draws on the register.
The quality charter carries sustainability checks on the Mossel Bay model (focus area 11). From 2029
climate risk and LTO funding also enter municipal IDPs, with reporting. The route exists: the Provincial
Treasury writes its district reviews to inform IDPs and budgets, and they already carry environmental risk
data.296

The register comes first, because routing, funding and capital choices all depend on knowing which sites
close and how often. Three districts then have positions to build on: the Cape Winelands as a climate-
smart wine region, the Overberg as a nature-positive test bed and the Garden Route as a case for
resilience investment.297

Why it can work
The register turns scattered losses into decisions. Some losses already carry a figure: weather
cancellations cost Robben Island roughly R16m a year, and the Meiringspoort rebuild costs R421m. Once
losses are dated and costed by site, Wesgro can route itineraries around a closure and operators can
weigh resilience spending against the loss it avoids. Stable funding works on the other half. An LTO with a

The evidence diagnoses this risk more than it prescribes for it. No comparator carries a climate-adaptation

financing system that supports conservation and sustains tourism demand. Valencia runs its SICTED
quality system with environmental compliance frameworks, and counts a stronger reputation for quality
and sustainability among the results. Mossel Bay's inspections show the same checks can work at town
scale. The condition is national. Until the effect of Circular 131 is resolved, the funding agreements the

THEME 3.1 · INVESTMENT, INCLUSION AND SUSTAINABILITY · FOCUS AREA 15


Crowd in capital with targeted investment strategies
The province's capital pipeline funds airports, ports, roads, rail and the Metro waterfront, but
almost none of it reaches regional product, bookability or community assets, where the
growth case needs investment.

The challenge
The growth case needs capital where the current pipeline does not reach. Visitors who travel beyond the
Metro need beds, a booking route and finished product. The communities need assets a local seller can
run. Yet committed capital closes the air gaps, not the regional ones (section 2.3). The province does not
lack capital works. It lacks capital aimed at tourism outside the Metro.

Tourism funding is also tight, and below peer destinations (focus area 14). Fiscal pressure is prompting
consideration of a tourism levy. The Department of Mobility sees a need for climate, development,
blended-finance and private-sector models to fund tourism-enabling mobility. Its draft land transport
framework goes further and plans its own business case for a tourist levy, to fund tourism transport. Much
of the product gap will therefore fall to private capital, which moves only where a case is ready. 300

The accommodation gap shows what is at stake. Province-wide, no mid-scale branded product sits
between luxury estates and basic self-catering, and the Garden Route is short of quality-graded hotels for
midweek, conference and business travel. Mossel Bay reports that limited upper-upscale hotel capacity,
particularly a recognised brand able to host conferences, holds back its higher-value and business
demand.301

The evidence shows a gap in direction as much as in volume. The district tables list a large capital
pipeline, and their gap columns show that none of it reaches regional product, SME bookability or
township product. That finding is an inference from the tables rather than a printed statement, and no
source gives a Western Cape tourism funding-gap figure [Western Cape tourism funding against the peer
set: DEDAT and Wesgro budget data needed]. Kliptown is the warning case: capital built before an
operator earned no operating income (section 2.1).

No scored list of tourism investment opportunities exists yet. The district gap-closure tables are its ready
input: each names a gap, where it sits and whether any capital is committed to it. Scoring them for return,
jobs and inclusion turns a list of needs into a ranked pipeline.

The course of action
Wesgro should build the tourism investment pipeline the gap tables imply, and commit public capital only
where demand has been shown. In 2027 and 2028 Wesgro's investment promotion team scores the gaps
for return, jobs and inclusion and prepares the first investment cases (section 4.3). These cover mid-scale
and conference-capable hotels in secondary nodes and accommodation at De Hoop and on Route 62.
From 2029 it takes the cases to investors. DEDAT, the Department of Mobility and the City of Cape Town
then test blended-finance vehicles and one levy case for the visitor economy. The pipeline is refreshed
each year.

One rule runs across the portfolio: invest after demand, and attach a maintenance and marketing
covenant to any public asset before it is let or upgraded. The order matters. A levy or a blended-finance
vehicle needs projects to fund, so both are tested only once the first cases exist. The levy also needs one
case, not two. Separate tourism and transport levies would charge the same visitors twice. DEDAT should
therefore agree with the Department of Mobility in 2027 that the Department's levy case covers destination
management as well as tourist transport. That is this report's recommendation, not a position either
department has taken.302

Why it can work
A scored pipeline lowers the cost of finding a deal. Investors see ranked cases with demand evidence
attached, rather than a list of district needs. Investing after demand reverses the Kliptown sequence, and
covenants keep a public asset maintained and marketed after it opens. Wesgro has brokered community-
linked capital before, though outside tourism. It brokered IDC funding for the Doringbaai abalone farm in
2015; the village trust holds 37% and the farm employs about 50.303

3.3.2 Governance and leadership
The second enabling theme covers who decides, who measures and how the plan is run. It holds five
focus areas. Three build the means of joint decision: three-tier alignment, collaboration with industry and
shared policy and data (focus areas 16 to 18). Two apply them to specific gaps. Focus area 19 escalates
the national deficits in infrastructure, visas and safety. Focus area 20 builds workforce skills on a labour
analysis still to be commissioned.

THEME 3.2 · GOVERNANCE AND LEADERSHIP · FOCUS AREA 16


Align institutional stakeholders across all three tiers
Air service agreements and visas sit with national government, mandates are split across
three tiers and the national marketing body is under strain, so the province needs one
shared agenda with clear decision rights.

The challenge
The levers the growth case needs are spread across three tiers of government, and none of them answers
to one plan. The national tier holds visa rules, air service agreements and route rights. The province holds
jobs, safety programmes and infrastructure planning. Local government holds basic services and precinct
management. The levers that bind growth most sit nationally (section 2.4). The province must therefore
move decisions it does not make.305

The bodies that should carry this work have thinned. The Department of Economic Development finds that
tourism institutional capacity has eroded over a decade at provincial and municipal level. Even working
partnerships lapse. FEDHASA reports that the Discover Garden Route MOU has expired, and that Knysna
municipality's non-participation limited its reach. Without one provincial voice, each ask reaches the
national tier from several directions or not at all.306

Source: Current State Diagnostic, Dec 2025 (Diag); Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026 (SIR; statements attributed to organisations, not

The evidence agrees on the problem and is thin on the fix. What is needed is "a shared growth agenda
with clear decision rights and joint delivery plans". No source maps decision rights by tier beyond one
summary table. The national tier is barely heard: the Department of Tourism and Stats SA spoke only to
data, and SA Tourism has no recorded view. The first task is therefore to hear the national tier directly and
write down who decides what. For the districts, one design is already on record. The Tourism Blueprint
2030 recommends tourism coordination at district level, with regional tourism organisations run as non-
profits, funded by district municipalities and seating the LTOs. It also asks for the LTO model to be set in
law. The Blueprint's targets were set before COVID, so this action plan treats its model as a position to
test, not a settled design. The mandate settlement below and the LTO funding model in focus area 14 test
it against Circular 131.307

The course of action
Wesgro should convene the shared agenda and hold the evidence the national tier needs, rather than try
to own levers it cannot. In the first quarter of 2027 the Wesgro CEO's office and DEDAT constitute a
Tourism Growth Steerco (section 4.2). Wesgro's CEO and DEDAT's Head of Department co-chair it, with
Cape Town Tourism as a standing member (section 4.7). Steerco agrees a decision-rights map by tier and
tables the first national escalation agenda (focus area 19). It gives the province one voice and one route to
national decisions. Steerco should also connect to the tourism coordinating cluster the City's Tourism
Development Framework proposed, if the City set it up.308

Route work runs alongside, inside existing agreements, through standing relationships with Indian carriers
and authorities and with China's state-owned carriers (section 4.4).309

Why it can work
Alignment works by settling ownership before it is contested. A decision-rights map shows each tier what it
owns, so provincial and local bodies stop waiting on decisions that are theirs to make. One forum then
speaks for the province, so national bodies receive one position rather than several. Where the
comparators name the body that negotiates air service agreements, it is national: Chile's civil aviation
board and Singapore's aviation authority. The province's lever is evidence and alignment, not authority. 310

Coordination across tiers works where it is permanent. A public-private structure led by Visit València

between national and state bodies. The condition is national engagement. The plan works only if the
national tier takes part in Steerco's agenda and answers its asks, which section 5.2 tracks. 311

THEME 3.2 · GOVERNANCE AND LEADERSHIP · FOCUS AREA 17


Strengthen industry and government collaboration
Fifteen of the sixteen organisations heard raised collaboration, and the channels already
exist in JAMS, the bid teams and the tour-operator forums, so the task is to turn existing
forums into one joint plan.

The opportunity
Collaboration is one of the two concerns stakeholders share most widely, and the channels for it already
work. Fifteen of the 16 organisations heard raised it, as many as raised data sharing (section 2.4).
FEDHASA reports that Western Cape tourism associations already coordinate through JAMS with
Wesgro, the Western Cape Government and the City of Cape Town. C-BRTA cites biannual tour-operator
forums, and the Department of Tourism cites statistics committees that already convene public and private
stakeholders. The province does not need another forum. It needs the forums it has to plan together. 312

Joint work already pays where the task is concrete. Mossel Bay's cruise calls rose from four before Covid
to 27 a season, supported by closer coordination and better excursion packaging. The town credits its
public-private tourism model with faster decisions. The CTICC finds the same on bids: destination
stakeholders collaborate effectively on bid development and international event attraction.313

Growth to 2035 needs that discipline applied to development, and here the system falls short. The West
Coast District Municipality finds provincial and regional forums dominated by destination marketing rather
than development. Operators are represented only through LTOs and business chambers, so private
participants are sometimes unaware of tourism plans and projects. The Department of Economic
Development reports that misalignment between public tourism institutions and key industry bodies is
weakening collaboration.314

The record describes a system that collaborates to sell and stalls when it must plan. Joint work succeeds
on a bid, a cruise call or a route, where the task is bounded and the payoff shared. It weakens on
development, where no forum owns the agenda and operators sit one step removed. The airlines and
distributors that fill the seats are also absent. No airline, online travel agency or tour operator gave a
recorded view.315

The course of action
Wesgro uses the forums that exist and gives them a development agenda and one joint plan. In 2027 it
adopts JAMS as the single channel to the associations, because FEDHASA expects consultation through
it to cascade more efficiently than approaching each body separately. It adds the Tour Guides
Association, so frontline operators have a voice. Sector councils for wine, business events, adventure and
culture are designed inside Steerco's structure (section 4.7) and run from 2029 (section 4.3). 316

Partners carry the regional and bidding work. District municipalities, DEDAT and the LTOs give regional
forums a development agenda and tailor provincial support to district needs. The CTICC and the
universities supply the academic and sector champions on whom international bids depend. Nationally,
the province asks SA Tourism to include provincial assets in its campaigns for emerging source markets,
which Robben Island Museum also seeks. From 2029 the joint industry plan is agreed and reviewed each
year.317

A joint plan gives each forum a decision to make, not just information to exchange. Sector councils give
wine, business events, adventure and culture a standing seat, so the market, not officials, sets
development priorities. Shared targets already pay here. Cape Town secured 36 conference bids in
2024/25, expected to bring more than 27,000 delegates and R745 million in economic impact through
2028. Cape Town also added 17 mainline international routes from 2015 to 2025, more than any of eight
secondary airports in the comparison. That record is credited to Wesgro's commercial and relationship-
driven route development.318

Valencia shows the model at destination scale. Sector-led executive committees, with the regional
business confederation on the governing board, keep tourism development market-driven rather than
bureaucratically led. Valencia received 3.5 million international visitors in 2024, 24.4% above 2019. That
performance is credited to governance that is decentralised, sector-led and embedded across the
destination. Singapore's tourism board, airport group and national airline work under one coordinated
strategy with shared KPIs. The condition is commitment from industry. The plan holds only if the

THEME 3.2 · GOVERNANCE AND LEADERSHIP · FOCUS AREA 18


Expedite tourism-enabling policy and data sharing
No approved method exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs, operators cannot see their
own customers and the arrivals series run unreconciled, so a data-sharing protocol is the
precondition for every target.

The challenge
The province cannot yet measure what the plan sets out to grow. The Department of Tourism reports no
approved method for provincial tourism GDP and jobs estimates. Official tourism GDP and employment
data have also lagged since Covid-era method changes, it adds. Data sharing draws more stakeholder
insights than any other theme, as section 2.4 shows. Bodies from Stats SA and SA Tourism to ACSA, the
airlines and the card schemes produce tourism data. Yet they collect different metrics at different
frequencies with different definitions.320

Every target to 2035 depends on fixing this. The plan tracks spend, season and spread beside arrivals. No
source yet prints visitor spend by district (section 5.3). The nearest measure is tourist spend as a share of
each district's GDPR. The Provincial Treasury's district reviews print it, from Wesgro and S&P Global
Market Intelligence data. Even arrivals are counted in ways that are not reconciled. Section 1.2 sets out
three measures: ACSA passenger arrivals, SA Tourism tourist arrivals and the growth model's
international arrivals. OAG's air series adds a fourth count. Without a shared data layer, no intervention
can be targeted or measured.321

Policy and the operators' own data are as thin. The Department of Economic Development reports a
provincial tourism policy gap, with limited legislation and out-of-date master-planning frameworks. At the
sites, third-party ticketing channels do not share visitor-level data with the Table Mountain Aerial
Cableway. Robben Island Museum cannot link its marketing spend to visitor acquisition.322

The evidence locates the gap in sharing, not in supply. Provincial macro-research is strong; what is

platforms to share it exist in JAMS, the statistics committees and a cross-agency task team. None of them

in progress, with owner and start date: Wesgro Research and DEDAT to confirm].323

Part of the gap is national and beyond the province's reach. Stats SA cites 749 vacancies as one factor
that appears to slow the tourism satellite account. The province can agree terms with data holders now. A
recognised provincial jobs and GDP method needs the Department of Tourism.324

The course of action
Wesgro's Research unit agrees data-sharing terms with each data holder and runs one indicator register
and dashboard, as section 5.5 sets out. In 2027 it builds the register and agrees terms with ACSA, SA
Tourism, Stats SA, Cape Town Tourism, the site operators and the booking platforms. It also agrees terms
with the Provincial Treasury for the data behind the Treasury's reviews and with the Department of
Mobility for its Integrated Transport Hub. In 2028 the dashboard goes live with automated arrivals, seat
and cruise feeds and quarterly digital tracking (section 4.3). The dashboard sets campaign results against
airline capacity and hotel trends, and annual or twice-yearly brand research sits beside it. Wesgro also
starts to measure meetings, incentives and exhibitions. Meetings are absent from consolidated provincial
statistics, and the scale of the other two is unverified.325

Partners and the national tier hold the rest. Attraction operators and CapeNature share visitor-level data
from their ticketing and reservation systems. DEDAT and the municipalities close the provincial policy gap
and embed tourism in IDPs and spatial plans, with reporting, as the West Coast District Municipality asks.
Nationally, the province asks the Department of Tourism to approve a provincial tourism GDP and jobs
methodology and Stats SA to resource the tourism satellite account.326

Why it can work
A protocol fixes definitions once, so the data the province already has begins to add up. The arrivals
measures can be bridged, so each is read against the others and the growth model is checked against
counted arrivals. Spend and spread gain a district baseline. Operators who see their own customers can
price, target and report, which gives them a reason to share. The dashboard also gives Steerco one set of
facts, so collaboration (focus area 17) argues over decisions rather than numbers.

New Zealand shows what shared data can support. Data shared between tourism bodies and airlines is
one of four enablers of its route-accountable marketing, with airport readiness, route incentives and co-
investment tied to route KPIs. That marketing reports more than 16,000 incremental bookings and a return
of about NZ$55 million in 2023/24. Valencia is the realism check: integrating data systems across
institutions there can create technical and organisational challenges. The condition is consent. Each data
holder must sign, so the number of agreements signed is the first measure of progress (section 4.3). 327

THEME 3.2 · GOVERNANCE AND LEADERSHIP · FOCUS AREA 19


Lead removal of infrastructure, visa and safety deficits
Visas, air rights, the runway and policing sit with national government, so the province leads
by escalation backed with evidence, and delivers the safety programmes and basic services
that are its own.

The challenge
Visas, air rights, airport capacity and policing shape how many visitors reach the province and how safe
they feel. The province holds few of these levers. National government holds visa rules, border
compliance and air service agreements. The province holds safety programmes and local government
holds basic services. ACSA, a national body, holds the runway (section 2.4). Escalation is therefore the
province's main tool for the national share.328

The deficits bear hardest on the markets the growth case needs. Chinese and Middle Eastern travellers
face slow, document-heavy visa processes. The Table Mountain Aerial Cableway cites visas as a
constraint on growth from India and recovery from China, and the CTICC on regional African events. 329

Inside the province, safety and basic services gate both visits and community growth. Nine of the 20
shortlisted communities record a safety-perception gap and five record extortion. A formal safety
assessment remains a separate requirement before any community product launches. Two provincial
documents and the City's framework name safety as a constraint, and the City's stakeholders rank it the
first priority for growth. The road from the airport is one concern. The Department of Mobility's draft
framework flags the N2 from Cape Town International as a congestion and safety problem for tourists.
Congestion costs the Metro an estimated R2.8 billion a year, and water scarcity acts as a hard ceiling on
visitor load in high-growth nodes. Here the province and the municipalities hold part of the answer. 330

Stakeholders raise the deficits widely. Ten of the 16 organisations raised them, and safety perception

decided.331

National reform can move arrivals. Ghanaian arrivals to South Africa reached 36,656 in 2024, a level the
source credits to the visa waiver without printing an earlier year [Ghana visa waiver start date, given as 1
November 2023 and as 2024: Wesgro Research to confirm]. E-visa expansion and the Trusted Tour
Operator Scheme are promising, but their effect on Western Cape arrivals is not yet proven. The
province's task is to make the case for reform in its own markets, with its own numbers. 332

The course of action
Wesgro runs a standing escalation agenda at Steerco. Each item names the decision, the national body
that owns it and the date the province asks for. The first agenda is tabled in 2027 (section 4.3). It covers
visa categories and processing for China, India, the Middle East and Africa. It also covers air service
agreements and immigration throughput at Cape Town International. The visa asks start from DEDAT's
visa regime study, which ranks published processing times first and visa exemption for China, India and
Nigeria second. The agenda takes the reforms that bear on visitors and first checks what has moved since
2024. Wesgro's Advocacy and Advisory unit was set up to build evidence-based policy feedback on levers
the province does not control. Once Wesgro confirms its remit, the unit runs the agenda day to day.
Research and Destination Marketing build the evidence for each ask, market by market. Stakeholder
insights already point to the evidence needed: the safety issues most damaging to demand and the
access barriers across air, visas and onward travel.333

The province delivers its own share in parallel. The Metro needs dedicated tourism safety units and
programmes. The City's framework for 2019 to 2024 proposed them. Its Tourist Safety Programme set out
secure zones, a Tourist Police unit, victim support and safety forums. From 2027 the City and the Western
Cape Government, with the other municipalities and SAPS, build on whichever parts run, once the City
confirms them (section 2.4). Safety compacts follow in pilot communities. Municipalities and CapeNature
fix basic services, water and connectivity at remote reserves and in tourism towns. Wesgro carries a
visitor-safety narrative in its marketing. It applies the four launch gates set in focus area 13 to any
community product it sells. From 2029 responses to the national asks are logged and followed up each
quarter.334

Why it can work
Escalation works when the ask is specific and the evidence is local. The method has already moved
decisions taken elsewhere. Cape Town added 17 mainline international routes from 2015 to 2025, more
than any of eight secondary-airport peers, a record credited to Wesgro's commercial and relationship-
driven route development (focus area 17). A standing agenda keeps each ask alive across budget cycles,
so a refusal becomes a follow-up rather than an end. It also gives coordination one anchor. Chile's
coordination is most powerful when anchored around a single strategic constraint, long-haul access.
Action on its own share, from safety units to basic services, strengthens the province's hand when it asks
others to act.335

Chile shows what national access policy can achieve. It runs one of the world's most liberal aviation
systems, using open-skies agreements, strong aviation institutions and private sector participation.
Passenger traffic at Santiago's airport rose from about 16 million in 2010 to more than 26 million in 2019.
For the Western Cape, the lesson is to lobby actively for air service liberalisation and simplified visa
regimes. Kenya reduces entry friction to position Nairobi as a regional hub. No comparator shows a region
escalating to its national government, so the province's own record is the first real test. The condition is a
national answer. The province cannot compel one, so it measures what it can: visa processing times in
priority markets and the share of its asks answered (section 5.2).336

THEME 3.2 · GOVERNANCE AND LEADERSHIP · FOCUS AREA 20


Develop workforce and service skills
The 300,000 tourism-linked jobs ambition has no baseline and no labour analysis behind it,
so the first step is to commission that analysis, and the skills plan follows from it.

The challenge
The province cannot yet count the jobs that growth is meant to create. The ambition of 300,000 tourism-
linked jobs is one of four headline ambitions, with no base year, baseline or target year. No labour
analysis sits behind it. The Department of Tourism reports that no approved methodology exists for
provincial tourism GDP and jobs estimates. This focus area therefore rests on analysis still to be
commissioned. Section 2.4 sets out why a jobs baseline must come before a jobs target.337

Jobs matter to the action plan as much as arrivals. Wesgro's mission is to drive inclusive and sustainable
growth and create jobs in Cape Town and the Western Cape. Service quality also rests on people. Mossel
Bay names inconsistent restaurant quality and service delivery as a major weakness. CapeNature is
cautious about scaling international demand because service readiness is uneven across its sites.338

Yet skills gaps surface only in passing. CapeNature finds low baseline skills in communities near reserves,
which need substantial upskilling before they can meet service standards. The West Coast District
Municipality reports stretched municipal officials, and the Overberg calls for substantial mentoring and
business support. Each is a statement, not a measure.339

starts, Wesgro Research extracts the tourism employment table from the Pero 2025. Until the analysis
reports, Chapter 5 carries jobs without a baseline or target. It sets the targets at the first gate, at the end of

3.4 The integrated case

The three pillars make one case. Demand work fills seats only if the seats exist, supply work
pays only if the product can be booked, and both need the enabling base to last.

The growth model sets out three paths from the 2025 base of 1,498,829 arrivals: 1,822,634 in 2035 on the
conservative path, 2,175,778 on the base path and 2,516,282 on the accelerated path. The paths differ by
intervention, not by demand potential. Each step of the growth case therefore rests on a named set of
interventions. The first two steps draw on Pillars 1 and 2 alone. Pillar 3 adds arrivals only on the third step,
through the three-tier agenda and national escalation (focus areas 16 and 19). Its other six focus areas
decide whether growth is shared, funded, sustained and measured.346

The conservative path, 1,822,634 arrivals in 2035, holds existing air access with no added frequencies or
routes and continues trade and marketing, with growth in the mature markets and current corridors. Focus
areas 5 and 7 hold that base. The base path adds 353,144 arrivals to reach 2,175,778. It assumes
Wesgro's trade and marketing continue at 2025 intensity while Emirates, Qatar, Ethiopian and Turkish
Airlines add frequency and capacity, and India, China and regional Africa grow through hub connections.
Focus areas 1 to 7, 9 and 10 hold it. The accelerated path adds a further 340,504 to reach 2,516,282. It
needs direct India and China routes secured and dedicated activation in India, China, the USA and the
GCC, with India, China and the Middle East growing ahead of their source markets. Focus areas 4, 7, 16
and 19 deliver it on top of the base.347

The gateway binds first, at peak. The growth model draws current air access as a line at 1.8 million
inbound seats, and the base case crosses it at the 2030 year point. Relief at Cape Town International
arrives about 2032 for the international terminal and about 2033 for the runway. ACSA now schedules the
realigned runway for December 2029, subject to contract award. This plan keeps about 2033 as its
planning date and treats the earlier date as upside. Between those dates, growth has to come from larger
aircraft and added frequency on the full routes and from hub-carrier capacity (focus areas 5, 6 and 7).
Domestic connections can also grow through George, whose terminal doubles by 2028 or 2029. 348

If the supply moves slip, growth flattens near the seat line. The conservative path, with no new seats,
ends at 1.82 million in 2035, just above the 1.8 million line. Projected growth cannot be realised without
added capacity or frequency, and without confirmed routes the accelerated case reverts to base. On the
full routes, demand work cannot make up the gap, because marketing raises fares rather than seats; on
the hub routes, which have seats to spare, it can. Wesgro's own target of 800,000 to 1 million new arrivals
over FY26 to FY30 sits above every modelled path (section 1.1).349

Below the gateway, the pillars depend on each other. Green-season demand needs seats in the
months carriers now cut (focus areas 2 and 7). Dispersal needs product that can be booked and reached
(focus areas 3, 9 and 10). Inclusion needs a seller and a safe visit before exposure (focus areas 13 and
19). Every target needs data that today sits with SA Tourism, ACSA, the Provincial Treasury and the
booking platforms (focus area 18).

Air access and bookability move first, because every other lever runs through them. Three phases
follow from that logic. Activate, 2027 to 2028, works on seats, bookability, green-season events, the
inclusion pilots and the governance base before the seat line. Expand, 2029 to 2031, scales hub
conversion, corridor product and quality and closes the first investments. Sustain, 2032 to 2035, takes the

Execution plan

4.1 From focus areas to initiatives
Twenty focus areas become eighteen initiatives, one per focus area except where one
owner delivers two.

The plan follows one chain from vision to measure. Each level answers a different question. The pillar
says what must change and the focus area says where; the initiative says what Wesgro and its partners
do, and the indicator says how Steerco will know it has happened (section 5.1).

Seven levels run from the vision to the measures, and each has a fixed count in this plan. One vision says
where the province is going: grow international arrivals from 1.5m in 2025 toward 2.5m by 2035. Three
pillars say what must change, such as Reshape demand. Six themes say in which part of the system,
such as source market growth and diversification. Twenty focus areas say where the evidence says to act,
such as Convert India and China hub traffic. Eighteen initiatives say what Wesgro and its partners do,
such as I4 India and China hub conversion. The 117 actions say who does what, by tier, such as
activation inside Xiaohongshu, Douyin and MakeMyTrip. The indicators, 7 at impact, 28 at outcome and
37 at output level, say how Steerco knows, such as T2-06 India and China hub arrivals. The counts come
from Chapter 3, the action tables and the traceability table in Appendix A1.

Two merges take twenty focus areas to eighteen initiatives. Segment-led marketing and mature-market
yield share one owner and one set of channels, so focus areas 1 and 5 form initiative I1. Institutional
alignment and industry collaboration share one forum, so focus areas 16 and 17 form I15. Every other
focus area has its own initiative, numbered I1 to I18 in one sequence so that the M&E toolkit can track
them on one list.

Leads are proposed, not agreed. They follow the Wesgro units named in the Inception Report's
stakeholder map and the actions in Chapter 3, and Steerco confirms them at its first meeting [Steerco to
confirm initiative leads and partners]. Six initiatives have a partner as the accountable lead, because the
main lever sits outside Wesgro's mandate: corridor mobility, quality standards, flagship sites, community
pilots, LTO funding and skills.

  Eighteen initiatives cover the twenty focus areas, and eleven of them are in implementation by 2028.

4.2 Sequencing and phases

Three phases run to 2035, and the first is weighted to the moves that change the 2030
constraint: seats, bookability, green-season events and the inclusion pilots.

The phases follow calendar years: Activate from 2027 to 2028, Expand from 2029 to 2031 and Sustain
from 2032 to 2035. Each phase ends at a decision gate tied to the Chapter 5 indicators (section 4.7). The
first gate, at the end of 2028, falls in Wesgro's FY2028/29, two years before the base case crosses the
model's seat line around 2030.350




Figure 4.1. Initiative timeline, 2027 to 2035
Source: Letsema, initiative portfolio (sections 4.1 and 4.3). Design means scoping, data, agreements and pilots; implement means delivery at
scale.


Phase 1: Activate, 2027 to 2028
Phase 1 works inside Wesgro's mandate and budget and on the levers that move before the airport can. It
holds and adds seats on the full routes, opens new routes outside the peak hours and builds the air
access fund (I6). It publishes the green-season calendar (I2), counts and starts to close the bookability
gap (I3) and starts two or three inclusion pilots (I12). Steerco, the data-sharing protocol and the escalation
agenda start in the first quarter (I15, I16, I17), because every other initiative reports through them.

The marketing initiatives (I1, I4, I5) and cruise (I7) spend 2027 on design: segment targets, hub-carrier
agreements, the Nigeria and Kenya plans and excursion products. Seven initiatives use both years for
design (I8 to I11, I13, I14, I18). Their first task is a baseline or a case that no deliverable holds: corridor
350
      Demand-Side Analysis, p21.


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                     131
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035                   DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



mobility, product, quality, flagship capacity, climate risk and LTO funding, the investment pipeline and the
labour analysis. The gate at the end of 2028 asks four questions. Have seats on the full routes held? Does
the bookability baseline exist and is it moving? Has any pilot met its thresholds? Which of Wesgro's FY26
to FY30 targets will the outputs meet (section 4.7)?

Phase 2: Expand, 2029 to 2031
Phase 2 scales what Phase 1 proved and meets the model's seat line. Hub conversion, regional Africa
and segment marketing run at scale. Corridor mobility and product move from design to delivery, on the
Garden Route, the Winelands and the Overberg first. The quality charter is adopted and phased by
district, and the investment pipeline reaches financial close on its first projects. The mid-term review in
2030 tests arrivals against the base path when the base case crosses the model's seat line, and checks
that the runway is on its published dates (section 5.6).

Phase 3: Sustain, 2032 to 2035
Phase 3 runs as relief arrives at Cape Town International: the international terminal about 2032 and the
runway about 2033. The direct India and China route cases built since 2027 go to carriers as capacity
frees up, which is the step the accelerated case depends on. Inclusion pilots that met their thresholds
scale through channels. Climate risk and LTO funding, written into IDPs from 2029, are reviewed each
year. The terminal evaluation in 2035 closes the plan (section 5.6).351




351
      Infrastructure Report, p103.


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                       132
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035                 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




4.3 Initiative charters

Each initiative has a one-page charter that a lead can run from and Steerco can hold to
account.

Every charter uses the same fields. It states the objective, the focus areas served, the proposed lead and
partners and the tier of government. It then sets the actions in each phase, marked Design or Implement,
and closes with the dependencies, the funding route, the risks and the indicators it moves. Actions come
from the Chapter 3 action tables. Leads are proposals for Steerco to confirm, and rand bands are set in
section 4.6.




Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                    133
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035                                       DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



I1 Segment-led activation and yield in mature markets
 Field                     Charter
 Objective                 Raise value per visitor from the mature markets and target every priority market by segment, not
                           by country alone.
 Focus areas               1, 5
 Proposed lead             Destination Marketing
 Partners                  Research; trade and OTA partners; SA Tourism; City of Cape Town; Cape Town Tourism
 Tier of government        Provincial (Wesgro); national alignment with SA Tourism
 Activate, 2027 to         Design (2027): segment targets inside each priority market; a shared segment frame with SA
 2028                      Tourism, the City and Cape Town Tourism; destination-specific segment research. Implement
                           (2028): move spend from broad awareness to trade-led conversion; luxury-advisor and high-end
                           operator distribution in the UK, USA, Germany, France and Switzerland.
 Expand, 2029 to           Segment campaigns at scale; spend per visitor and length of stay added to Wesgro's own targets;
 2031                      segments built into brand tracking.
 Sustain, 2032 to          Segment research refreshed; budget shifted toward the segments and markets with the highest
 2035                      yield.
 Dependencies              I6 for seats on the full routes; I16 for spend-per-visitor data; I10 for product quality.
 Funding route             Main: Wesgro operating budget. Supporting: private capital. Rand band to be set (section 4.6).
 Risks                     Carriers on full routes raise fares rather than seats; SA Tourism's institutional weakness limits joint
                           campaigns (Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026, p25).
 Indicators                T1-01, T1-02, T1-03; T2-01, T2-07; T3-01 segment targets in Wesgro's annual plan; T3-02 share
                           of market budget in trade-led conversion; T3-37 international tourism trade engagements by
                           market.

 Expand, 2029 to           Regional conferences in Stellenbosch and George; lifestyle events at CTICC in December and
 2031                      January; flower, fynbos and whale events marketed earlier.

 Risks                     Weather limits summit and outdoor events (Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026, p35); peak

I13 Climate resilience and destination management, including LTO funding
 Field                     Charter
 Objective                 Track and plan for climate losses, and give local tourism bodies stable funding with a development
                           mandate.
 Focus areas               14
 Proposed lead             DEDAT, with Wesgro Research
 Partners                  Municipalities; district municipalities; CapeNature; site operators; Provincial Treasury
 Tier of government        Provincial and local; national for MFMA Circular No. 131

4.4 Roles and accountabilities

Wesgro is accountable for twelve initiatives and does part of the work in all eighteen; for the
other six, the accountable lead sits with another tier, and the plan names it.

The matrix below assigns each initiative to the four roles: responsible (R) for doing the work, accountable
(A) for the result, consulted (C) before decisions and informed (I) of progress. Where Wesgro is
accountable and does the work itself, the cell reads A/R. Assignments are proposals for Steerco to
confirm, built from the actions in Chapter 3.

National government and the state-owned entities
Four levers sit outside the province and cannot be bought or marketed into place. Route rights and visas
sit with the national tier, the airport and port capital programmes with national SOEs and policing with


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                152
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035                                         DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



SAPS. The plan treats them as an escalation agenda (I17), carried by Steerco through DEDAT to the
national departments and SOEs. Wesgro's Advocacy and Advisory unit, set up to build evidence-based
policy feedback on levers the province does not control, is proposed to run it day to day. DEDAT's visa
study supplies its visa asks, already ranked. Each item is stated as a decision, an owner and a date the
province is asking for. Wesgro's own route work (I6) runs inside the existing agreements meanwhile.352

Provincial government
DEDAT is a standing member of Steerco and the accountable lead where the lever is policy or funding
rather than promotion: quality standards (I10), LTO funding and climate risk (I13) and the labour analysis
(I18). The Department of Mobility leads the transport half of corridor mobility (I8), including the airport leg
with the City and ACSA. It also joins the levy case (I14) and brings its Integrated Transport Hub into the
data protocol (I16). The Provincial Treasury's district reviews feed the data protocol and the IDP step (I13,
I16). Wesgro's units lead the demand, access, product and data initiatives and report through the
programme office.

District and local government
Municipalities and LTOs hold the assets, the selling bodies and the visitor services that the regional and
inclusion initiatives depend on. They lead the community selling bodies in the pilots (I12), attach
covenants to public assets (I12, I14) and carry the route and product work in their districts (I3, I9). The City
of Cape Town is a partner on segments, events, routes, cruise, mobility, product, flagship sites,
community pilots and safety (I1, I2, I6 to I9, I11, I12, I17). Cape Town Tourism is a partner on segments,
routes and data (I1, I6, I16), and both sit on Steerco. The City's framework for 2019 to 2024 proposed
three plans the initiatives can build on where they run. They are an air, rail and cruise access programme,
a Cape Town+ offer and a Tourist Safety Programme.353

The private sector and site operators
Airlines decide frequency and gauge, hotel investors decide the mid-scale tier and attraction operators
manage their own capacity. The plan gives them the evidence and the demand case. It asks for the
decisions it cannot make for them: seats on full routes (I6), investment in secondary nodes (I14) and
visitor-flow management at flagship sites, where the operators are accountable (I11).

4.6 Funding and investment

Most of the plan runs on budgets that already exist; the capital it needs sits with ACSA,
PRASA, Transnet and private investors, and none of it has been costed yet.

No deliverable costs any initiative. The Inception Report scoped an investment opportunity framework with
return scoring, but no separate output was delivered, and stakeholders describe Western Cape tourism
funding as materially below peer destinations without giving a figure. Thirteen initiatives draw mainly on
Wesgro's operating budget, and four need private capital as a main source; the initiatives table in section
4.1 gives each one's main and supporting funding routes. This section leaves the rand bands to be set
with Wesgro Finance and DEDAT [cost band per initiative and phase: Wesgro Finance and DEDAT to set
before Steerco signs off the plan]. Wesgro Finance and DEDAT also agree a stop list before Gate 1,
naming the Wesgro work deprioritised to fund the initiatives (section 4.8). [Open item: Wesgro Finance to
give an indicative rand band for the first 24 months.]354




Figure 4.2. Initiatives by main funding source
Source: Letsema, count of initiatives by main funding source from the initiatives table in section 4.1. An initiative can have more than one
main source.

Crowding in capital. The rule for capital is the Inclusion Plan's: invest after demand is shown, and attach
a maintenance and marketing covenant to any public asset. Wesgro's investment promotion work builds
the tourism pipeline from the Infrastructure Report's gap-closure tables, where every district lists tourism
gaps with no committed capital (I14). The air access fund scales Cape Town Air Access's existing route
fund with public and private money (I6). Its likely funders already sit in that partnership: the City, the
province, ACSA, Cape Town Tourism, SA Tourism, Wesgro and private partners. Corridor mobility (I8)
bids into the provincial transport budget; the draft land transport framework allocates R22.8bn to transport
initiatives in the 2024 MTEF. The same framework plans a tourist levy case, which I14 would widen to
cover destination management.355
