What the SLA asks and what is still to do. The SLA asks for return projections on infrastructure

costed projects, and the only costed items in hand are the airport, rail and port programmes the province
does not fund. The return case is therefore an output of I14 in Phase 1, built project by project as the
pipeline forms.

4.7 Delivery governance

One Steerco, one programme office and four fixed decision points keep eighteen initiatives
and three tiers of government on one plan.




Figure 4.3. Delivery governance structure
Source: Letsema, proposed delivery governance.

Steerco. A Tourism Growth Steerco co-chaired by Wesgro's CEO and DEDAT's Head of Department,
with Cape Town Tourism as a standing member and the City of Cape Town, the district municipalities and
the industry bodies at the table. It is distinct from the Unlocking Tourism Steerco that oversees this study.
It receives the quarterly scorecard, clears cross-initiative issues, holds the escalation agenda and
recommends gate decisions to the Board. Stakeholders asked for this kind of alignment directly: mobility
responsibilities alone are split across three tiers of government. Steerco should also connect to the
tourism coordinating cluster the City's framework proposed, if the City set one up. 356

Programme office. A small office inside Wesgro runs the plan on a build, operate and transfer basis. It
builds the charters, the dashboard and the reporting calendar in Phase 1, runs them through Phase 2 and
hands them to the line units by the end of 2031. It owns the scorecard and the dashboard, and Wesgro
Research owns the indicator definitions and the data protocol (I16).

Cadence. Initiative leads report monthly to the programme office, Steerco meets quarterly and the Board
receives an annual outlook, which also anchors the Unlocking Tourism business outlook event. The plan
is refreshed each year against the indicators and the gate decisions.

4.8 Risks and dependencies

5.1 Results chain
Every initiative is measured at three levels: what it produces, what changes because of it
and what that adds to the 2035 case.

The framework is the written form of the M&E toolkit the SLA requires (clauses 1.1.4 and 1.1.10). It sets
indicators, baselines and targets for arrivals, spend, jobs, airlift, cruise and SMME inclusion, with the tools
to track them [clause wording to be checked against the signed Annexure B]. The Inception Report adds
geographic spread and asks for definitions, sources, frequency, owners and governance for each
indicator.358




Figure 5.1. Results chain for the plan
Source: Letsema, results chain for the plan.

The table below gives the chain for each initiative, pillar by pillar. Tier 3 output indicators come from the
initiative charters, Tier 2 outcome indicators from the focus areas and Tier 1 impact indicators from the
growth case.

      (I6 to I11) move seats, cruise volumes and the share of visitors and spend outside the Metro. Pillar 3
      initiatives (I12 to I18) move community spend and jobs, and supply the governance and data every other

5.2 Indicator set

Seven impact indicators, twenty-eight outcome indicators and thirty-seven output indicators
measure the plan, each with a definition, a source, an owner and a frequency.

Tier 1 measures the 2035 case and is reported to the Board. Tier 2 measures the focus areas, one to
three each, and is reported to Steerco each quarter where the source allows. Tier 3 measures the
initiatives' own outputs and is reported by the leads to the programme office each month. The full register,
with targets, sits in the toolkit (section 5.7).

5.3 Baselines and targets

The arrivals targets come straight from the growth model, while the jobs and other Tier 1
targets wait for baselines that do not yet exist.

Baselines are fixed at 2025, the growth model's base year, for the growth-model indicators and at
FY2024/25 for the airport series, the recovery baseline used in Chapter 1. Targets follow two paths: the
base case, which assumes Wesgro's current activity continues with hub carriers adding capacity, and the
accelerated case, which adds direct India and China routes and dedicated activation. The model prints the
base case for 2028, 2030, 2032, 2034 and 2035, and the other paths for 2035 only. The 2031 base value
and the accelerated milestones are computed and marked as such.359




Figure 5.2. Arrivals paths and milestones to 2035
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p19 (2025 base); Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p20, p21 (2028 and 2035 points; 1.8m
seat line). The 2031 base value is the geometric mean of the printed 2030 and 2032 points; the accelerated and conservative milestones are
computed at a constant rate between the printed 2025 and 2035 values.

Table 5.5. Tier 1 baselines and targets

      Arrivals carry targets on both paths; spend carries the working analysis's base path; jobs and the other

Arrivals. The model's 2025 base and the airport series measure different things: arrivals on the growth
model's basis, which Skift is to confirm (section 1.1), and international passenger arrivals at Cape Town
International. The airport series is the quarterly proxy because ACSA publishes it, and it stood at 1.44m in
FY24/25 and 1.57m in FY25/26. Both are reported, each against its own history. 360

Wesgro's targets. Wesgro's FY26 to FY30 plan sets five-year levels for four indicators. Three of them sit
below the recent pace and the fourth holds it. T2-11 counts calls toward 250, about 50 a year, against 79
vessel visits in 2024/25, because its base appears to leave out the 2023/24 reroutes (section 1.1). T2-02
counts bids secured toward 161, about 32 a year at about R28m each, against 36 at about R20.7m each
in 2024/25. T2-09 counts first-time scheduled international routes toward 10, two a year, against about
three a year since 2015 on Cape Town Air Access's count. It tests each route on load factor and survival.
T3-37 counts trade engagements toward 12,500, about 2,500 a year and flat on FY24. Each indicator
therefore reports against Wesgro's level and its own baseline, and the action plan's target is the higher of
the two. Wesgro's 800,000 to 1 million new arrivals runs above every modelled path to 2030 (section 1.1),
so T1-01 keeps the model's targets and reports Wesgro's beside them.361

Spend. T1-03's path holds spend per arrival fixed, so its targets are in constant 2024 rand, while SA
Tourism reports nominal rand; progress is read after deflating by CPI. The model's 2024 spend of
R27.12bn sits 19% above the R22.7bn SA Tourism reported for FY2024. [Open item: Skift to rebase the
spend path on the FY2025 actual of R25.9bn.]362

Jobs. No deliverable carries a provincial tourism employment baseline, and the Pero tourism table could
not be retrieved [provincial tourism employment baseline: Wesgro Research to supply from the Pero 2025
or the Stats SA provincial TSA]. T1-04 therefore carries no baseline or modelled path until the labour
analysis sets a provincial measure (I18).

The report restates the 300,000 as a total-sector figure for the labour analysis to test (I18). For context,
public sources put direct tourism employment at 106,707 in Cape Town in 2024 and 953,981 nationally in
2024. The City's framework counts about 45,000 direct and 150,000 total tourism jobs in Cape Town. Its
360
    Demand-Side Analysis, p11.
361
    Wesgro, Strategic Plan 2025/26 to 2029/30, p20, p59; Diagnostic, p37; Demand-Side Analysis, p36, computed; Cape Town Air Access,

362
    Market Assessment, p10, p24.

direct count probably rests on a narrower definition than the 106,707; the labour analysis reconciles the
two.363

Green season. The 2025 month table sums to 104.9%. Normalised, it gives 61.9% for October to March
and 38.1% for the green season, in line with the printed 61% and 39% for 2024. T1-05 uses 38.1% as its
baseline.364

Spread and community spend. No deliverable prints district visitor spend. The Provincial Treasury's
district reviews print tourist spend as a share of GDPR, from 6.7% in the Metro to 16.8% on the West
Coast in 2023. That measures tourism's weight in each district economy, not where provincial visitor
spend lands. The share outside the Metro therefore has no baseline until the data protocol delivers one
(I16), and T1-06 carries no interim level. The community share starts from the Inclusion Plan's traced floor;
its 1% is a reference point the plan prints, not a target any party has adopted. 365

5.4 Data sources and gaps

Arrivals, seats and cruise volumes can be tracked from existing feeds; spread, bookability,
jobs and community spend cannot until new data is built or shared.

Stakeholders put data among the top barriers: no approved methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP
and jobs, and no one holds a single view of visitor behaviour. The data-sharing protocol under focus area
18 (I16) is the route to closing the gaps below.366

5.5 Tracking mechanism

A quarterly scorecard to Steerco, an annual outlook to the Board and a live dashboard
where the sources allow it.

Four principles run the tracking. Automate before collecting by hand: arrivals, seats and cruise volumes
come from feeds that already exist. Name one owner for the dashboard (the programme office) and one
for the definitions (Wesgro Research). Report every indicator against its own baseline and both paths.
Refresh the register each year at the plan review.




Figure 5.3. Quarterly scorecard layout
Source: Letsema, dashboard concept. Illustrative layout; no values shown.

Reporting runs on a fixed calendar tied to Steerco and the Board. Each month, the leads send Tier 3
outputs, initiative status and the seats and arrivals feeds to the programme office as a dashboard update.
Each quarter, the programme office takes Tier 2 outcomes where available, the risks rated high and the
escalation responses to Steerco in the scorecard. Each year, Steerco takes Tier 1 impact against both
paths, the plan refresh and gate status to the Board in an outlook report, made public at the business
outlook event. At each gate, Steerco sends the Board a gate paper on the tests in section 4.7.




Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                    173
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035                                           DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




5.6 Evaluation and learning

Two evaluations bracket the plan: a mid-term review in 2030, when the base case crosses
the model's seat line, and a terminal evaluation in 2035.

The mid-term review tests whether the plan is on its path and whether the path still holds. It falls in 2030
because that is when the base case crosses the model's 1.8 million seat line, before runway relief on the
planning date. The review also falls at the end of Wesgro's FY26 to FY30 plan period, so it reads the
plan's six targets against the result. The terminal evaluation in 2035 measures the result against both
paths and sets up the next plan. Both should be run by a party independent of the programme office. 367

Each pillar carries two evaluation questions, one on results and one on the assumptions behind them. For
Reshape demand: did arrivals, spend per visitor and the green-season share move with the activation,
and in which markets and segments? Is conversion still the constraint, or has access overtaken it in the
growth markets? For Transform supply: did seats, readiness and bookability rise outside the Metro, and
did visitors follow? Did the airport relief arrive on time, and did the second gateways carry the load
meanwhile? For Build enabling capability: did the pilots meet their thresholds, and did community spend
rise? Did the national asks get answers, and did the data protocol deliver the baselines?

Pilots before capital. The inclusion pilots are evaluated before any capital is committed, on the Inclusion
Plan's own measures: bookings, visitors and locally retained revenue. A pilot scales only when it meets
the thresholds agreed before launch. The same rule applies to any product or asset investment under I9
and I14: test demand, then invest.368

Learning into the plan. Findings from each review, gate and pilot go into the annual plan refresh under
section 4.7, so the charters, targets and sequence change with the evidence rather than at the end of the
plan.


5.7 Toolkit handover

The M&E toolkit turns this chapter into four working files that Wesgro Research owns from
handover.

The four files are the indicator register, the baseline workbook, the scorecard template and the dashboard
data model (SLA clauses 1.1.10.2 and 1.1.15.3) [clause wording to be checked against the signed
Annexure B]. The indicator register holds all 72 indicators with their definition, unit, source, frequency,
owner, baseline and target by year and path. The baseline workbook holds the baseline values with page
references to the deliverables, and the computed values with their method. Wesgro Research owns both.
The programme office owns the scorecard template, which carries the quarterly Steerco scorecard and
the annual Board outlook. It also owns the dashboard data model: the feeds, refresh frequency and
manual-entry forms for the dashboard in section 5.5.

Handover runs through knowledge-transfer sessions with Wesgro Research and the programme office, as
the SLA requires (clause 1.1.15). The Excel toolkit itself is outside this report and is listed as an open item
[M&E toolkit workbook: Letsema to deliver with the final report].

A2 Demand model

The demand model takes international arrivals from 1,498,829 in 2025 to 1,822,634,
2,175,778 or 2,516,282 in 2035, and its base case crosses the 1.8 million seat line the
model draws, at the 2030 year point.

Method
The model sets a growth rate for each source market from four levers and projects arrivals from the base
year to 2035. The levers are source market growth, Western Cape penetration of source-market
outbound, an airlift capacity ceiling and structural market conditions. Source-market growth sets the
ceiling: "WC cannot sustainably grow faster than it's source market is generating travellers". Projections
are anchored on OAG aviation data and Skift global traveller data.369

Scenario definitions
The three scenarios vary intervention, not demand: "The difference between scenarios is not demand
potential, but the extent to which structural barriers are removed and growth opportunities are actively
pursued".370

The 2030 and 2035 values equal each group's arrivals times its average spend, so spend per arrival within

the same deck reports foreign direct spend of R22.7bn in FY2024. The June deck prints no spend
projection.376

Seat line and model limits
Both decks state that "Current air access places a hard ceiling at 1.8 million inbound seats, meaning the
projected growth cannot be realised without additional capacity or frequency". Neither deck prints the
seats behind the line or a load factor that converts seats into arrivals. At the international routes' 76.0%
load factor, 1.8 million seats would carry about 1.37 million arrivals, below the 1.57 million recorded in
FY25/26. This report therefore reads the line as the model's assumption, not a measured cap (section
1.3).377

The model prints its seat line and six base-case year points but no seat table, annual series or group split
for the other scenarios. The seat line is 1.8 million inbound seats, drawn as a line labelled 1.8M and called

annual series: 2026, 2027, 2029, 2031 and 2033 are absent, and the June chart starts at 2024, not 2025.

no per-market rates.379

The calendar or financial basis and the source series of the 1,498,829 base are not stated, and the 2025
bars are not marked as actuals or model values. ACSA passenger arrivals (1.57m in FY25/26), SAT
tourist arrivals, OAG air arrivals and model arrivals are not reconciled.380

The two ambitions do not reconcile with each other or with the measured baseline. The 2.8 million names
no series or base year. The 5.7 million arrivals and R54.7 billion of spend trace to the Tourism Blueprint
2030, which set them before COVID as a goal over ten years. The Diagnostic dates them to 2035; this
report treats that statement as superseded and leaves the year open. Twice the 1.44 million international
passenger arrivals at Cape Town International in FY24/25 is 2.88 million, close to the first and far from the
second.381

The model's 2025 base of 1,498,829 sits just below South African Tourism's count of 1,538,142 for 2025.
Yet each market group's 2025 value tracks its 2024 value grown at the model's own rate. The report
therefore treats the base as a model value until its basis is confirmed.382

The airport count for FY25/26, 1.57 million, sits above the model's 2025 base and below its 2028 base-
case point of 1,673,855. Yet the two differ in measure and period, so the gap is no evidence that the
model is cautious.383

A3 Benchmark cases

Six comparators were drawn from a 30-destination screen on twelve criteria, and each case
reduces to a system type, two levers and the changes they require of the Western Cape.

Both the Inception Report and the Global Benchmarking Report screen destinations on twelve criteria in
four groups. Each destination received one average score across all criteria.384

How the six were chosen
The screen scored 30 destinations on the twelve criteria and ranked them on one average score. Chile,
Melbourne and Victoria, Valencia and Singapore came from the top of the ranking. New Zealand, ranked
seventh at 3.67, and Kenya, ranked eleventh at 3.50, joined for what the score could not weigh: a remote
single-gateway case and an African case with community-based tourism.385

System type and its three components
The report treats sustained growth as the product of three reinforcing components: institutional
coordination; strategic marketing and promotion; accessibility and connectivity. Without competitive
access, "latent demand does not convert".387

All six cases align coordination, marketing and access, but each builds its system around a different
anchor.388

Melbourne and Victoria: events-led. Coordination institutionalises events and a knowledge-led brand as
a cross-government economic development tool. Marketing integrates city branding, intellectual capital
and events into a single demand proposition. The event calendar is used to de-risk and stimulate aviation
demand.

Chile: connectivity-led. Coordination is anchored around a single strategic constraint, long-haul access.
Marketing runs unified national branding with defined regional identities and multi-region itinerary
packaging. Liberal aviation policy and major hub investment position Santiago as a single efficient long-
haul hub.

Kenya: product-led. Coordination aligns tourism with national priorities in conservation, trade and
diplomacy. Marketing is segment-specific and grounded in a designed product system. Access reduces
entry friction, with Nairobi as a multipurpose regional and global hub.

Singapore: orchestrated experience. A centrally orchestrated system under STB works through
structured public-private partnerships. Marketing packages experiences (events, precincts, lifestyle) into
seamless high-value propositions. Aviation, infrastructure and visitor experience are integrated into a
single seamless gateway.

Valencia: governance-led. Governance is decentralised and sector-led. Marketing expands the reasons
to visit: gastronomy, culture, sport, wellness and business events. Connectivity supports a multi-product
destination system and dispersal.

New Zealand: value-led. Tourism, aviation and policy are integrated around shared demand objectives.
Marketing shifts from volume to value by selling scarce, high-quality experiences. Long-haul demand
converts only when marketing, aviation and distribution are aligned.

Levers and required changes
Each case closes on a transferable-levers slide that names two levers and the change each requires
locally. The table gives those bullets in the report's words, shortened only by cutting. 389

A4 Readiness scores

The infrastructure assessment classifies 50,332 assets in six districts and scores each
district on attractiveness, readiness and accessibility; overall ratings fall from 4.5 in the Cape
Metro to 1.9 in the Central Karoo.

Method
The assessment models tourism supply in three layers (attractions, services and infrastructure) and holds
that "destination performance is constrained by the weakest layer". Its asset database sorts the layers into
twelve categories with named subtypes. It draws on Wesgro, SANParks, StatsSA and CapeNature data,
with Overpass, WikiQuery and tourism reports for 2023 to 2025 named as sources of geographical data
points.390

Table A.14. Asset database structure

 Layer                      Category                      Subtypes
 Attraction                 Natural                       Beach; mountain; park & reserve; scenic routes
 Attraction                 Cultural & heritage           Museum; historical site; monument; cultural precinct
 Attraction                 Commercial and leisure        Wine estate; theme park
 Attraction                 Events                        Festival; sporting event; cultural event; business events
 Service                    Accommodation                 Hotel; guest house; lodge; self catering
 Service                    Food & beverage               Restaurant; café; bar
 Service                    Tourism services              Tour operator; travel agency; car rental
 Service                    Visitor amenities             Fuel station; retail centre; visitor information centres
 Infrastructure             Air access                    Airport; airstrip
 Infrastructure             Road network                  National route; regional road
 Infrastructure             Transport nodes               Bus terminal; train station
 Infrastructure             Public services               Hospital; police station
Source: Infrastructure Report, Jul 2026, p10, as printed in sentence case.

The comparative tables score a different set of eleven categories in three groups, and the district
scorecards chart a similar set. Attractions cover activity, commercial & leisure, cultural & heritage, events
and natural assets. Services cover accommodation, eatery and shopping. Transport and enabling
structures cover transport, enabling structure and visitor amenities. The asset counts use five groups:
accommodation, attractions, eateries, events and amenities.391

Attractiveness carries 40% of the stated weight and readiness and accessibility carry 30% each.
Attractiveness and quality asks how compelling the tourism offer is, through visitor ratings, uniqueness,
price positioning and experience depth. Readiness and capacity asks whether the region can service and
convert demand, through capacity and digital discovery. Accessibility asks whether visitors can reach and
move through the destination, through airport proximity, gateway travel time and public transport
availability. The scale runs from 1 'Very weak' and 2 'Weak' through 3 'Functional' and 4 'Strong' to 5
'Leading'. The stated method is: "Average indicator scores within each dimension, then combine
dimensions using the agreed weights".392

Each dimension is the average of its indicator scores, and the dimensions are then combined at 40/30/30.
All three use a common scale from 1 to 5, with Functional at 3 and Leading at 5. The report states four

assumptions. Asset volume does not equal readiness, and one framework applies to all regions. Indicator
scores are evidence informed rather than purely formula derived, and missing data is not treated as poor
performance.393

Overall rating. The overall ratings are the Infrastructure Report's evidence-informed judgement, not a weighted
formula, so this report does not re-derive them.

Touring routes
Each of the six touring routes has a route card with four metrics and a scorecard that rates four readiness
dimensions. Ready means the route "meets international expectations now", Developing that it is
"workable, with notable gaps" and Gap "a binding constraint to address". The method slide announces five
dimensions but lists four, numbered 1, 2, 4 and 5. The West Coast overall score of Emerging is not one of
the three defined ratings.397

A5 Inclusion fact base

The Inclusion Plan scores 107 long-listed communities down to a shortlist of 20, rates their
readiness, sizes the spend they could capture and takes its order of action from five
benchmark cases.

Long-list rule and scoring weights
The long-list takes every Census 2011 sub-place cluster with 1,500 people or more and official
unemployment of 25% or more, plus named mission, fishing and farm settlements. The rule yields 107
township and rural communities with 2.81 million people in 2011 (48% of the province) and 3.79 million on
the 2025 estimate. Of the 107, 93 sit within 45 minutes of one of 35 supply-report nodes or three
corridors.399

By region the long-list holds Cape Metro 26, Cape Winelands 16, Overberg 19, Garden Route 23, West
Coast 13 and Central Karoo 10. The shortlist takes five from Cape Metro and three from each other
region. Need rests on Census 2011 because Census 2022 is not published below municipality and ward
and asked no income question.400

      Need carries the largest weight of the five criteria, and each criterion scores a community from 3 to 1
      against printed thresholds.

the p14 panel prints 18 shortlisted, three per region, against 20 on p3, p16, p18 and p28. Drive times are OSRM free-flow estimates (p16,
p17).


Archetypes and responses
Two tests set the archetype: a 45-minute drive time to the nearest node or corridor and whether any
attraction sits inside the boundary. Atlantis is held remote by Wesgro naming at 26 minutes from Darling.
For the largest group the plan's diagnosis is that "the product and the node's demand are there; the seller
is missing".401

Organisation 11, Attraction 6, Safety 1, Market link 1 and Infrastructure 1. The p28 headline counts six
communities with asset damage against five Gap cells. The plan names no pilot community and assigns no
owner; it tests two to three of six profiled exemplars and lists stakeholders for validation only. 405


Spend capture scenarios
The 20 communities hold 18.4% of Western Cape residents but a documented floor of R4m to R10m a
year in visitor spend, while 1% of FY2025 foreign direct spend would land R259m. They also hold 15.4%
of employed residents; both shares come from Census 2011. Foreign direct spend traced to businesses
trading inside the communities is a floor of 0.02% to 0.04%, which the plan documents as R4m to R10m
landing in the 20 communities a year.406

At 1%, FY2025 foreign direct spend would land R259m in the communities, which implies R25.9 billion of
FY2025 foreign direct spend (computed). At 1% of the 2035 spend ambition the figure is R547m, which
implies R54.7 billion of spend in 2035 (computed). The plan cites the Demand-Side Analysis (16 Sep
2026) and Diagnostic Report v2 for that scenario. The Diagnostic sets the ambition as 5.7 million

      Inclusion Plan, p27, computed, p20 to p26, p40 to p51.

      Inclusion Plan, p27, p53 to p58, p32.

international arrivals and R54.7 billion of tourism spend by 2035, a different case from the 2.5 million this
report carries. Both figures trace to the Tourism Blueprint 2030, which set them before COVID as a goal
over ten years. Appendix A2 sets out the demand model's own 2035 cases.407

Benchmark cases and the order of action
The five cases point to one order of action: "a seller with a stake, a safe and workable visit, product that
keeps spend in the community, then exposure". The plan's six takeaways are that a sustainable local
operator is essential, resident ownership matters, safety is a prerequisite, local spend should be captured,
existing product should be sold and spending should be tracked.408

The plan finds that local selling mechanisms lasted while assets and training without a durable route to
market did not, but Costa Rica's selling network did not survive donor exit.409

Rio de Janeiro, Santa Marta and Rocinha. The Rio Top Tour's first phase cost about R$230,000
(R$184,000 federal, R$46,000 state). The Cantagalo elevator cost about R$89 million and the Providência
cable car R$75 million in a R$163 million package, and almost 10,000 officers were deployed at peak. The
tours drew about 4,500 visitors in their first month in 2010, and about 20 resident guides were working by
2015. In 2014, 81% of foreign visitors spent up to R$10 in Rocinha. The cable car stood idle for eight
years and security collapsed in 2017, yet Rocinha drew 544,000 visitors in 2023. Access and safety were
preconditions, not product, and resident guides continued after the programme that launched them. 410

Medellín, Comuna 13. Metrocable Line J (2008) cost about 96.9 billion pesos and the escalators (2011)
about 10 billion pesos, with maintenance above 260 million pesos a year; the integrated urban programme
passed 100 billion pesos by 2020. Comuna 13 drew 1.66 million visitors in 2022 and 1.98 million in 2025,
52% of them international, with more than 1,000 businesses and about 300 guides. A 2023 study put its
capacity at 667 visitors a day. The lesson is to fund a mandate, a capacity ceiling and a community body
alongside the escalator; residents developed the product first.411

Soweto, Vilakazi Street. The Hector Pieterson Museum cost R23.2 million, the Mandela House
restoration about R9 million from corporate donors and Orlando Stadium about R280 million; the public-
realm cost is not disclosed. The street drew about 1,000 tourists a day by January 2010. Sakhumzi grew
from four employees in 2001 to about 110 in 2019, and the ten restaurants provide more than 800 jobs,
though the Mandela House company was liquidated. Upgrades amplified existing assets but benefits
concentrated on street-front owners, so design governance and a resident precinct entity belong at the
outset.412

Costa Rica, ACTUAR. UNDP GEF small grants funded the network from the 1990s. Law 8724 (2009)
gave ICT duties without a budget, and no state funding for the selling network has been identified.
ACTUAR, founded in 2001, turned over more than USD 800,000 in 2013 for more than 30 member
communities and dissolved at the end of 2018; its benefits "stopped at association members". A
management entity with its own selling channel was the engine, and it did not survive donor exit. 413

Soweto, Kliptown. Kliptown received R436 million in its first allocation. The JDA spent about R497 million
from 2001 to 2010 plus R40 million to 2013, Walter Sisulu Square cost R160 million to R180 million and a
48-room hotel R24 million. The business case projected almost 200,000 international visitors a year, but
the museum drew about 4,000 a year by 2017 and the hotel traded above break-even only during the
2010 FIFA World Cup. Local small businesses took 5% of construction contract value, and the site was


407
    Inclusion Plan, p5; Diagnostic, p5; Tourism Blueprint 2030, p4, p10.
408

A6 Stakeholder inputs

Phase 1 engaged 16 organisations, whose 223 rated insight statements span all three pillars
of the hypothesis tree and inform eight priority areas for deeper engagement.

Engagement record and method
Engagement runs in four phases: Intro & Framing from January to March 2026, sense-making and
validation from April to May, socialisation of draft recommendations from June to July and mobilisation
from August into October. The March deck closes phase 1 and marks phase 2, a validation round with a
select few stakeholders, as still to commence. [Phase 2 to 4 records not in hand: Letsema to supply the
organisations engaged, session dates and outcomes for each phase.]415

The project team held structured sessions with key stakeholders, extracted insights, mapped each to the
project hypothesis tree and aggregated them. Each insight is a concise statement written by the team, not
a verbatim quotation. Every row carries a pillar, a category of the tree and one impact rating of High,
Medium or Low. The ratings are not defined, and no session dates, participants or roles are recorded, so
this appendix attributes insights to organisations and does not quote them.416

Organisations listed for later phases
The deck lists 13 supply-side stakeholders, 6 proposed demand-side engagements and 11 enabling-
environment stakeholders engaged or in progress. No airline, online travel agency or tour operator is
among the 16 phase 1 organisations.417

The later-phase list names the demand-side voices that phase 1 lacks, but marks all six demand-side
engagements as proposed. The 13 supply-side stakeholders, seven of them with a phase 1 table, are:
Tourism offices of each District; Table Mountain Aerial Cableway; Robben Island Museum; Hospitality
Providers; Conference Venue Operators; CapeNature; SANParks; CBRTA; Ikhwa ttu; CCID; CTICC;
FEDHASA; V&A Waterfront.418

The 6 proposed demand-side engagements, none with a phase 1 table, are: Trip.com Group (China,
ASEAN); Thomas Cook India (India, Middle East outbound); DNATA Travel (Middle East, India, ASEAN);
China International Travel Service (China); Emirates (China, India, Middle East, ASEAN); SATSA. The 11
enabling-environment stakeholders engaged or in progress, five of them with a phase 1 table, are:
Department of Economic Development and Tourism; Ministry of Mobility; South African Tourism;
SouthAfrica.net; We Do Tourism; SATOvito; Statistics South Africa; Google (Government Affairs and
Public Policy); Cape Town International Airport; Winelands Airport; George Airport. The appendix tables
print SATVITO, C-BRTA, !Khwa ttu and Cape Nature.

SANParks, the V&A Waterfront, the CCID, hospitality providers, conference venue operators, South African
Tourism, SouthAfrica.net, We Do Tourism and the three airports have no phase 1 table. The entry for district
tourism offices overlaps the Mossel Bay, Overberg District and West Coast District Municipality tables. 419

A8 M&E toolkit

The toolkit register adds the source, frequency, owner and target to each Tier 2 indicator in
section 5.2.

Targets come from the growth model where it prints them and from the Inclusion Plan where it sets a pilot
count. Wesgro's FY26 to FY30 plan sets them for bids, routes and cruise calls. Elsewhere the target is a
direction or a milestone, marked proposed where it is this report's choice; Steerco fixes the levels at Gate
1 once the 2027 baselines exist. Tier 1 targets are in section 5.3 and Tier 3 outputs in the charters.

Scorecard and dashboard templates
The quarterly scorecard carries one row per indicator: code, name, baseline and year, latest value and
date, direction against the path, target, source and owner. The annual Board outlook adds the Tier 1 chart
against both paths and the gate status. The dashboard data model lists each feed, its refresh frequency
and the manual-entry form for indicators without a feed. The templates themselves are delivered with the
Excel toolkit [M&E toolkit workbook: Letsema to deliver with the final report].

A9 Glossary and references

Eleven terms carry a specific meaning in this report, and thirty-nine documents support it,
cited by the date on their covers.

Abbreviations are listed in the front matter. Footnotes cite each deliverable by a short title, keyed below;
the table gives its full title and the date printed on its cover, and where the file date differs, both are shown.
Working analyses rank below the deliverables. Wesgro's, the province's and the City's own documents are
cited directly by page, slide or chapter. Other public sources are used only where no deliverable carries
the figure.

Table A.27. Glossary of terms

 Term                        Meaning in this report
 Accelerated case            The growth model's upside path: direct India and China routes secured with dedicated activation;
                             2,516,282 arrivals in 2035
 Base case                   The growth model's most likely path: Wesgro's current activity continues and hub carriers add
                             capacity; 2,175,778 arrivals in 2035
 Cold zone                   The Infrastructure Report's term for an area with tourism assets but little or no tourism use
 Focus area                  One of the twenty entries on the hypothesis tree, each with a Chapter 3 entry, an initiative and
                             indicators
 Green season                April to September, the months of lower overseas demand
 International arrivals      On the growth model's basis, which Skift is to confirm; distinct from international passenger
                             arrivals at Cape Town International, which ACSA reports
 New route                   Wesgro's term, in its FY26 to FY30 plan, for a scheduled international service flying to and from
                             Cape Town for the first time; counted in T2-09
 Readiness                   The Infrastructure Report's score for how ready a district's assets are to receive visitors, beside
                             attractiveness and accessibility
 Seat line                   The 1.8 million inbound seats the growth model draws as current air access, as drawn in the
                             Demand-Side Analysis; a model assumption, not a measured cap (section 1.3)
 Seller with a stake         The Inclusion Plan's term for a local body that sells community product and keeps the revenue in
                             the community
 Tier 1, 2 and 3             Impact, outcome and output indicators (section 5.2)
Source: Letsema, from Chapters 1 to 5.
